The confirm flow needed one supporting change: `api.js`'s generic error handler previously stringified any non-string `detail` object into `[object Object]`, which would have made the 409's actual match data unreachable by any caller -- fixed by attaching the parsed `.status`/`.detail` onto the thrown `Error` alongside the existing stringified `.message`, a general fix any future structured-error endpoint can also use, not an allergen-specific hack. `MealPlanEntryRow`'s "We made this" button now catches a 409, renders the conflict via `RestrictionWarnings`, and offers "Confirm anyway" (resends with `acknowledge_restriction_conflict: true`) or "Cancel". `HealthPage.jsx`'s household-preferences form gained a checkbox grid (fetched from `GET /household/allergen-options`, a new tiny endpoint exposing `allergen_service.ALLERGEN_CHOICES`/`OBSERVANCE_LEVELS` so frontend and backend can't drift apart) and a gluten-observance-level `<select>` that only appears once gluten is checked.

**Deliberately not done.** No NLP/synonym expansion or compound-ingredient decomposition ("may contain traces of" disclosure text isn't parsed, "enriched flour" isn't expanded to name wheat unless "flour" is itself a keyword, which it is) -- this stays a keyword/regex system, consistent with the project's "never let the LLM invent/guess, but also don't pretend a deterministic keyword match is smarter than it is" discipline; documented as a known limitation in the module docstring. No per-member restrictions (see above). No recall-awareness (that's B3.3, a separate backlog item).

**Verification.** 25 new unit tests (`test_allergen_service.py`; 93 total in the repo, all passing) covering basic matching, case-insensitivity, multi-allergen/multi-ingredient combos, the gluten-superset behavior, word-boundary compound-word avoidance, unknown-key handling, every negation case (including the two regression-fixing cases above), cross-contact gating on both conditions independently, `check_ingredients`' combined-category behavior, `to_dict()` shape, the DB-backed `check_household_restrictions()` wrapper (no-preferences-row, persisted-preferences, and empty-allergen-list cases), and taxonomy self-consistency. Live end-to-end pass against a real local uvicorn instance covered every wiring point: `PATCH /household/preferences` persisting `restricted_allergens`/`gluten_observance_level` and rejecting an unknown allergen key with 422; creating a recipe with "wheat flour" (restricted), "rolled oats" (cross-contact only), and "gluten-free vanilla extract" (correctly NOT flagged, confirming the negation logic) and confirming `GET /recipes/{id}` returns exactly the expected `restriction_warnings`/`cross_contact_warnings`; creating a meal-plan entry against that recipe and confirming `POST .../confirm` returns 409 with the full match payload, then succeeds and sets `is_confirmed: true` once resent with `acknowledge_restriction_conflict: true`. Frontend: `npm run build` succeeds cleanly (built from a `/tmp` scratch copy per the standing FUSE-mount note) with no transform errors across all five touched/new components. **Not independently re-verified this pass**: the recipe-import preview's warnings and the meal-plan-generation preview's warnings were verified by code inspection and the shared unit-test coverage of the underlying `allergen_service` functions they call, not by a live curl through those two specific endpoints (import needs a live Ollama the sandbox can't reach; generation likewise) -- same standing limitation as every prior AI-surface feature in this project. Actual on-screen appearance of the new banners/checkboxes was not visually inspected in a browser, same open item as B10.4/B10.6's responsive/print passes.

## Capstone research: best-in-class landscape (2026-07-31)

Competitive/market research pass done *before* looking at Chef's own code, deliberately, so the findings would not be anchored on what already exists. This section records what the field looks like; the gap items it produced live in the Backlog section above. Sources are listed at the end of this section.

### What the incumbent meal planners are each good at

| Tool | The thing it does better than everyone else |
|---|---|
| Paprika | Recipe capture and organization; excellent web clipper, one-time purchase, no subscription. Deliberately not smart -- no AI, no pantry awareness, no plan generation |
| Mealime | Speed and zero setup overhead; short ingredient lists, fast grocery list, ~7M users, genuinely usable free tier. Dinners only. Pro tier adds a "Meal Prep Day" batch-cooking mode |
| Eat This Much | Automated macro/calorie-target-driven generation; merges cook events so leftovers reduce stove time. Thin on variety and inspiration |
| Plan to Eat | Calendar planning and leftover scheduling as first-class concepts; scale servings, reschedule, track frozen meals |
| Samsung Food (ex-Whisk) | AI recipe personalization -- convert a recipe to vegan, rebalance it nutritionally, rewrite it around in-stock ingredients, adjust cook time or skill level. Vision AI calorie estimation from photos (Galaxy devices only). Pantry management exists but is basic and paywalled |
| Tandoor (self-hosted) | The most feature-dense open-source option: barcode scanning into OpenFoodFacts, supermarket aisle mapping, meal cost calculation, URL import across 500+ sites |
| Mealie (self-hosted) | Polish and family usability; clean Docker deployment, strong URL import, good UI |
| Grocy (self-hosted) | Whole-household inventory discipline -- pantry stock, consumption tracking, chores, equipment -- with recipes as one module among several |

Chef's intended position sits between Tandoor's data richness and Eat This Much's automation, with a local LLM and health-metric feedback loop neither of them has. The gaps worth closing are mostly Tandoor's data-layer features (barcode, aisles, cost) and Plan to Eat's / Mealime Pro's planning mechanics (leftovers, prep day), not the AI layer where Chef is already ahead.

### Nutrition data services

- **USDA FoodData Central** -- free, government-backed, ~300K+ items, full nutrient profiles, API key required. No barcode lookup, weak branded coverage. The correct authoritative base layer.
- **Open Food Facts** -- free, open, crowd-sourced, ~2.5M+ packaged products, barcode-native. Best complement to USDA for branded/packaged goods, and the right choice for an app shipped to self-hosters.
- **Nutritionix / Edamam / Spoonacular / FatSecret** -- aggregate USDA plus branded and restaurant data, with genuinely better NLP (Edamam's is purpose-built for user-submitted recipe text) and restaurant-menu coverage (Nutritionix). All paid past a low free tier; Edamam scales to ~$999/mo and Nutritionix enterprise starts around $1,850/mo. Rejected as a dependency for this repo -- fine as an optional user-configured integration, wrong as a requirement for anyone cloning it.
- **USDA FoodKeeper** -- machine-readable feed of storage/shelf-life guidance for 500+ foods, and the source app now surfaces FSIS/FDA recall notifications.
- **USDA FSIS Recall API and openFDA food enforcement API** -- both free and public; openFDA covers 2004-present, updated weekly.

### Nutrition standards worth encoding

- **DRIs** (EAR, RDA, AI, UL) from the National Academies are the reference values the Dietary Guidelines are built on -- the right basis for per-member daily targets from age/sex/height/weight/activity.
- **HEI-2020** -- 0-100 diet quality score, 13 components (9 adequacy, 4 moderation), scored against the Dietary Guidelines. US population average is 58/100. Computable, published methodology, gives an objective plan-quality number.
- **Portfolio diet** -- the pattern specifically engineered for LDL reduction (plant sterols, viscous fiber, nuts, soy protein), overlapping with but more targeted than DASH or Mediterranean. Directly relevant to the author's stated goal. DASH additionally targets sodium (1,500-2,300 mg/day) and lowers LDL and triglycerides; Mediterranean lowers LDL while raising HDL.
- **RAG grounding for nutrition advice is an active research area**, and the recurring finding is that LLM systems which do not encode population reference-intake distributions risk producing recommendations without appropriate nutritional constraints. Chef's knowledge/RAG layer is the right shape for this; it just ships empty (see B2.1).

### Celiac and allergen tooling

Fig and Spoonful are the reference implementations. Two capabilities stand out and neither is present in Chef: a user-set **observance level** (must avoid gluten and cross-contamination is not ok / must avoid gluten but cross-contamination is ok / trying to avoid), and **cross-contact flagging** that goes beyond the ingredient list -- e.g. flagging non-certified oats and shared-equipment risk rather than only matching the word "wheat."

### Inventory capture, food waste

The whole category (NoWaste, Your Food, PantryWise, ShelfSmart, Recipy, NourishMate, GroceryTracker Pro, Dietrack) has converged on the same three mechanisms: **barcode scan**, **receipt OCR** (leading implementations claim 95%+ line-item accuracy on standard supermarket receipts, translating SKU codes into full product name/UPC/category), and **photo recognition**. On top of that, universally: color-coded expiry status, expiry-sorted lists, and **notifications ahead of expiry** (commonly 7 days out and on the day). Chef has photo recognition and urgency scoring but neither barcode nor receipt capture, and no notification of any kind.

### Cooking-time UX

Cook mode with the Screen Wake Lock API (user-activated toggle, battery-impact warning), step check-off, inline kitchen converters (cups/grams, F/C, pan size), step timers, and PWA installability with offline access -- this cluster is standard now and Chef has none of it. WCAG 2.1 AA is the accepted benchmark for PWAs; large-type high-contrast rendering matters more than usual for a screen being read across a kitchen with messy hands.

### Recipe interchange

**Schema.org Recipe JSON-LD** is the de facto interchange format -- Google's recommended structured data, published by most recipe sites, and what enables read-aloud/assistant guidance (needs `recipeIngredient` and `recipeInstructions`). Parsing it directly is faster and more accurate than LLM extraction from prose. **Cooklang** is the notable plain-text alternative. Public recipe-scaling APIs exist that handle smart culinary fractions and unit promotion.

### Health data integration

CGM data reached consumers in 2026 -- Withings ingests Abbott Lingo readings via Apple Health / Health Connect and, as of Q1 2026, a direct API. Aggregator APIs (Terra and similar) cover 500+ wearables including CGM data types but are paid and cloud-dependent, which fits a local-first self-hosted app poorly. File-based export import from Apple Health / Google Health Connect is the pragmatic first step.

### Grocery pricing reality check

Kroger publishes a public developer API for its own stores; Walmart publishes no public price API; Instacart pricing is reachable mainly through third-party scraper services. The honest 2026 assessment of AI recipe apps is that they are price-blind unless they hand the cart to a real merchant that returns live pricing. For a self-hosted app distributed to strangers, scraping is not an acceptable dependency -- user-entered or receipt-derived pricing is the deliverable, retailer APIs at most an optional per-user integration.

## Model selection: default Ollama chat/vision models updated (2026-07-31)

The author asked whether Fiduciary's models (they run `qwen3.6:27b` + `bge-m3`) should replace Chef's original defaults (`qwen2.5:14b` chat, `llava:13b` vision), and whether a newer Qwen release than 2.5 should be used at all. Checked Fiduciary's actual code rather than trust the paraphrase (`docs/DEPENDENCIES.md`, `.env.example`, `portfolio-api/llm.py`) -- confirmed it uses `qwen3.6:27b` (dense, tool-calling required for its agentic Strategist) and `bge-m3`, and confirmed it has **no vision model anywhere in its stack** (grepped for vision/image/llava/qwen-vl config, only generic-English-word false positives came back). Chef's `ollama_embed_model` default was already `bge-m3`, matching Fiduciary -- no change needed there.

Web research (Alibaba's own published benchmarks plus several 2026 model-comparison writeups; the specific quality claims below rest on vendor/blog-reported numbers, not independently reproduced) found: `qwen3.6:27b` is a real April-2026 dense-27B release, strong on coding/agentic-tool-use benchmarks (SWE-bench, Terminal-Bench) -- the right fit for Fiduciary's workload, but a poor default for Chef's on this specific hardware. At 17GB it doesn't fit a single 11GB GTX 1080 Ti; Ollama's multi-GPU support (automatic since v0.11.5) would split it across both cards, but tensor-splitting only lets a bigger model fit -- it does not parallelize a single request, so cross-GPU communication over non-NVLink PCIe likely makes generation *slower*, and it would occupy both GPUs per request, defeating the author's own hardware-section intent that the second 1080 Ti stay free for a concurrent second model. `qwen2.5vl:7b`, by contrast, was found to meaningfully outperform LLaVA-family models specifically on structured/document visual content (labels, receipts, charts) -- directly relevant to Chef's vision use cases (inventory photo intake now, receipt OCR B4.2 later) -- while being smaller (6GB vs. 8GB) than `llava:13b`.

**Decision (author-confirmed)**: switched the seeded defaults to **`qwen3.5:9b`** (chat/meal-plan/recipe generation -- a newer Qwen generation than 2.5, 6.6GB, fits one 1080 Ti with headroom, reportedly strong on general knowledge/instruction-following/multimodal benchmarks even against much larger models) and **`qwen2.5vl:7b`** (vision). `qwen3.6:27b` was deliberately NOT made the default -- documented in `settings_service.py`'s new description text as a reasonable upgrade path on beefier/single-larger-GPU hardware, same optional-override pattern Fiduciary itself uses for its own heavier model via `ANALYSIS_MODEL`. The author is removing `qwen2.5:14b` and `llava:13b` from their Ollama instance since they're no longer the target defaults.

**Changed**: `SETTING_SPECS` defaults in `settings_service.py` (chat -> `qwen3.5:9b`, vision -> `qwen2.5vl:7b`, with the reasoning captured in each spec's `description` so it surfaces in the Settings UI itself, not just here), `.env.example`'s optional first-run seed values, and `README.md`'s prerequisites section. These are **defaults for a fresh install only** -- an existing database that already seeded `qwen2.5:14b`/`llava:13b` keeps those values until changed via the Settings page (by design: `seed.py` never overwrites an existing setting).

**Not done, worth a follow-up**: no empirical A/B test of `qwen3.5:9b` vs. the old `qwen2.5:14b` was run against Chef's actual prompts (recipe-import JSON parsing, meal-plan generation) -- the recommendation rests on published general-purpose benchmarks, not Chef-specific verification, and no live Ollama reaches Claude's sandbox to run one. Fiduciary has a `scripts/model_ab.py` pattern worth mirroring here once the author has both models pulled and can run a real comparison.

## Multi-theme support: Fiduciary themes + Catppuccin (2026-08-01)

Author-requested, direct follow-up to noticing Fiduciary has a Settings-page theme section. Asked for that project's themes ported in alongside Chef's existing default palette, plus Catppuccin (catppuccin.com) as its own option; when asked how many of Catppuccin's four "flavors" to include (Latte/Frappé/Macchiato/Mocha), the author chose all four rather than just the flagship Mocha.

**Investigated Fiduciary's actual mechanism before porting anything** (via a research-only subagent, since Fiduciary's frontend turned out to be a single vanilla-JS/HTML file, not React as assumed going in -- the *mechanism* still ported directly, only the JS glue needed adapting). Fiduciary uses plain CSS custom properties overridden via a `data-theme` attribute on `<html>`, with `localStorage`-only persistence and a synchronous pre-hydration apply to avoid a flash of the wrong theme on load. Chef adopts the same `data-theme` mechanism, but with two deliberate departures given the project's own persistence requirement ("content should be persistent across container rebuilds") and existing infrastructure: (1) the real source of truth is a new DB-backed `ui_theme` setting (one new `SettingSpec` entry, no schema change -- `AppSetting` is already a generic key/value table, and `seed.py`/the existing `GET`/`PATCH /system/settings/{key}` endpoints handle it with zero additional code), with `localStorage` kept only as an instant-apply *cache* for the flash-avoidance trick, reconciled against the DB value once the app mounts (`App.jsx`'s new effect); (2) Chef's ~10 color vars (`--color-bg/-surface/-text/-text-muted/-primary/-primary-hover/-secondary/-accent/-danger/-border`) were already the semantic layer with no separate Tier-1-primitives/Tier-2-semantic split the way Fiduciary's `--bg`/`--amber`/etc. needed -- so each theme is one flat `:root[data-theme="key"]` block overriding exactly those 10 vars, nothing renamed. A genuine advantage found along the way: Fiduciary's own overlay/rgba tokens needed manual per-theme restating because raw `rgba(r,g,b,a)` literals don't recompute from a hex var -- Chef never had that problem because every derived tint in `theme.css` already uses `color-mix(in srgb, var(--color-x) N%, transparent)` (established in the B3.1/B3.2 warning banners and B1.2's provenance badges), which resolves `var()` live, so all of those automatically recolor correctly under every new theme with zero extra per-theme work.

**Ported four Fiduciary themes verbatim** (colors copied from `portfolio-api/static/index.html`'s own `:root[data-theme]` blocks, not re-invented): `amber` (Fiduciary's own dark-terminal default -- an additional selectable option here, not Chef's default), `cobalt`, `highcontrast`, and `daylight` (Fiduciary's one light theme, whose color choices in the source file are themselves the product of two rounds of the author's own live contrast feedback per that file's comments -- inherited as-is rather than re-litigated). Fiduciary's `--bd2`/second-border-strength tier and `--dim`/third-text-fade tier have no Chef equivalent (Chef has one `--color-border` and two text tiers, not three) and were dropped rather than added net-new just to preserve every source value.

**Added all four Catppuccin flavors** (`catppuccin-latte/frappe/macchiato/mocha`, hex values from catppuccin.com/palette, fetched live rather than from training-data memory). Catppuccin's own token names (Base/Mantle/Crust/Surface0-2/Text/Subtext0-1/Mauve/Green/Red/Peach/Lavender/etc.) don't map 1:1 onto Chef's semantic vars, so a mapping was chosen and applied identically across all four flavors rather than improvised per-flavor: `bg`=Base, `surface`=Surface0 (Mantle for Latte specifically -- Latte's lightness ramp has nothing brighter than Base to serve as an "elevated card" tone the way Surface0 is lighter-than-Base in the three dark flavors, so Mantle's slightly-darker/warmer tone is the closest available separation), `border`=Surface1, `text`=Text, `text-muted`=Subtext0, `primary`=Mauve (Catppuccin's own most iconic/branding hue -- it's literally the site's `<meta name="theme-color">` value for Mocha), `primary-hover`=Lavender, `secondary`=Green, `danger`=Red, `accent`=Peach (the closest analog to Chef's own default accent's "warm highlight" role). This mapping choice is documented directly in `theme.css`'s comment block, not just here, so a future flavor addition (Catppuccin doesn't currently have a fifth) has a documented convention to follow.

**New files/changes**: `frontend/src/themes.js` (the JS-side registry -- `THEME_OPTIONS` with per-theme swatch-preview colors grouped by family, plus `applyTheme()`/`getCachedTheme()`); `frontend/index.html` gained a small inline, un-deferred `<script>` before `<div id="root">` that reads the cached theme from `localStorage` and sets `data-theme` synchronously, mirroring Fiduciary's own "apply as early as possible" comment; `App.jsx` gained a mount-time effect that fetches `GET /system/settings`, and if the DB's `ui_theme` differs from the cached value (e.g. changed from a different browser/device), reconciles by re-applying and re-caching; `SettingsPage.jsx` gained a new "Appearance" card (swatch-card grid, grouped by family, click-to-apply-and-save) ahead of the existing generic settings list, which now excludes `ui_theme` from its own plain-text-field loop so the field isn't editable two different ways; `theme.css` gained the 8 new `:root[data-theme]` blocks plus `.theme-swatch-*`/`.theme-group-label` picker styles, following the existing `.status-dot`-adjacent-block placement convention.

**Deliberately not done**: no per-theme override of spacing/radius/font-sans -- matching Fiduciary's own explicit design decision that only color primitives are themed, confirmed still appropriate here since nothing in the request asked for structural changes, only color schemes. No validation of the `ui_theme` value against the known-key set on the backend -- consistent with every other `SETTING_SPECS` entry today (none are value-validated, only key-validated via `is_known_key`), so an unrecognized value just falls through to `themes.js`'s own `VALID_KEYS` guard (silently coerces to `"default"`) rather than the API rejecting it; tightening this uniformly across all settings would be a separate, broader change.

**Verification**: `npm run build` succeeds cleanly (built from a `/tmp` scratch copy per the standing FUSE-mount note) with 52 modules transforming without error; grepped the built, minified CSS to confirm all 8 `data-theme` selectors survived minification intact, and confirmed `chefTheme` (the localStorage key) is present in the built `index.html`'s inline script. Live end-to-end pass against a fresh migrated+seeded DB and a real local uvicorn instance: `GET /api/system/settings` returns `ui_theme` with its seeded default of `"default"`, and `PATCH /api/system/settings/ui_theme` with `{"value": "catppuccin-mocha"}` persists and round-trips correctly. **Not independently verified**: actual on-screen appearance of any theme (no browser available) -- same standing limitation as every prior UI-only change in this project (B10.4, B10.6, B3.1/B3.2's frontend). Worth a real visual pass across a few themes (especially confirming the Latte/daylight light themes and the high-contrast theme actually read well, since those are the ones most likely to expose a contrast mistake) before considering this fully done.

## B1.4 notes: daily/weekly nutrient roll-up vs. DRI targets (2026-08-01)

Picked as the next item after the theming work, per the author's "continue with the next item on the list" -- the highest-priority unclaimed item in B1 ("Nutrition data integrity," the backlog's own top-priority group), and one of the four options already on the table when the author picked B3.1/B3.2 instead two sessions ago.

**New `app/services/dri_service.py`** computes each household member's daily nutrient targets from age/sex/height (on `HouseholdMember`)/weight (most recent `HealthMetricEntry`, via a new `get_latest_weight_kg`)/activity_level -- fields that existed since Phase 6 and were, before this, never used for anything beyond BMI and a chart. Every numeric guideline used is sourced and cited inline in the module docstring, checked live rather than assumed from training data (the 2025-2030 Dietary Guidelines for Americans were released January 2026 and are current as of this writing): calories via Mifflin-St Jeor BMR x an activity multiplier; protein at 1.2 g/kg (the low end of the 2025-2030 DGA's revised, debated 1.2-1.6 g/kg range -- chosen over the higher end since the stated household context is sedentary, and over the older 0.8 g/kg RDA since that's now documented as a deficiency-prevention floor rather than current DGA guidance); carbs/fat via the decades-stable IOM AMDR ranges (45-65%/20-35% of calories, midpoints used); saturated fat and added sugars both capped at <10% of calories per DGA (unchanged in the 2025-2030 edition); sodium capped at the DGA's flat 2,300 mg/day; fiber via IOM's age/sex-banded Adequate Intake (38g/30g men under/over 50, 25g/21g women under/over 50); cholesterol at a documented 300mg *reference* value, explicitly flagged as NOT a current official DRI/DGA figure since the DGA has published no specific numeric cholesterol cap since 2015 ("as low as possible") -- chosen because it's a common, defensible clinical reference point directly relevant to the author's own stated LDL-reduction goal. Returns `None` plus a `missing_fields` list (e.g. `["weight"]`) rather than ever guessing a target from incomplete data -- the same "never silently invent a number" discipline as B1.2's provenance tags.

**`meal_plan_service.compute_nutrition_summary(meal_plan)`** sums each non-skipped, recipe-assigned entry's `Recipe.nutrition` (always per-serving, per `food_data_service.compute_recipe_nutrition`) into per-day and week totals. A entry's nutrition is added exactly once regardless of `entry.servings` -- `entry.servings` scales how much gets cooked/deducted from inventory (`compute_grocery_list`), not how much any one person eats, and the per-serving figure already represents one person's portion, which is the right unit for a per-person total. Explicitly documented known simplification: this assumes every household member eats exactly one serving of every planned meal -- there's no per-person meal-attendance or portion tracking anywhere in the schema to do better. Each day also reports `entry_count` vs. `contributing_entry_count` (a recipe that's never had nutrition computed or estimated doesn't silently vanish from the day, nor does it get blended in as a fake zero -- the gap is visible).

**New `GET /api/meal-plans/{plan_id}/nutrition-summary` endpoint** combines the two: per-day/week totals plus every household member's target (or `missing_fields` if incomplete), so a household member missing body data still shows up in the response with an explanation rather than being silently dropped.

**Frontend**: new `NutritionSummaryPanel.jsx` (same self-contained, `planId`/`refreshKey`-driven pattern as `GroceryListPanel.jsx`) added to `MealPlanPage.jsx` between the weekly entry grid and the grocery list -- a table of per-day + week totals across the same 9-key nutrition set used everywhere else in the app (`food_data_service.NUTRITION_KEYS`), a per-member target list, and a simple "week calories as % of 7-day target" figure per member for a quick at-a-glance read without requiring the user to do the day-to-week-to-percentage math themselves.

**Deliberately not done**: no per-member intake tracking (the household-wide, one-serving-per-meal assumption above is a real, stated limitation, not an oversight -- building real per-person attendance/portions would be a materially bigger schema change with no existing UI concept to hang it on); no visual warning/coloring when a week's totals exceed a cap (sodium, saturated fat, added sugars) -- the raw numbers are shown side by side with the target and left for the user to read, rather than this feature also deciding what counts as "bad enough to flag," which risks conflicting with B3's already-established allergen-conflict-blocking UI pattern if done hastily. Both are reasonable, scoped follow-ups rather than gaps in this item itself.

**Verification**: 27 new unit tests (18 for `dri_service.py` -- BMR hand-calculations, every missing-field/fallback branch, fiber age/sex banding, the DB-backed latest-weight lookup; 9 for `compute_nutrition_summary` -- unscaled-by-servings behavior, same-day summing, week summing, skip/no-recipe exclusion, the entry-count-vs-contributing-count honesty check, sort order, null-value handling; 120 total in the repo, all passing). Live end-to-end pass against a freshly migrated+seeded database and a real local uvicorn instance: two household members (one with full body data, one missing weight+height), two recipes (one with nutrition data, one without), a 3-entry meal plan spanning two days -- confirmed day totals, week totals, the 2-entries-but-1-contributing case rendering both counts honestly, Jason's full DRI target computing correctly, and the second member's response correctly reporting `daily_targets: null` with `missing_fields: ["weight", "height"]` instead of being silently omitted. Frontend `npm run build` succeeds cleanly (53 modules, built from a `/tmp` scratch copy per the standing FUSE-mount note). **Not independently verified**: actual on-screen appearance/layout of the new table (no browser available, same standing limitation as every other UI-only change this project).

## B10.5 notes: Imperial/Metric/Weight unit toggle, density-sourcing (2026-08-01)

Picked as the next backlog item after B1.4, since its stated blocker (B1.1's food-database resolution layer, for the weight-mode density case) was already satisfied and it's within the higher-priority B10 (author-requested) group -- the same "pick the lowest-effort item with no remaining blocker" logic used to pick B10.6 over B10.5 itself a few sessions ago, now inverted now that the blocker is gone.

**`unit_conversion_service.py` gained `convert_for_display()`**, the shared per-view rendering layer both B5.3 and B10.5 were always meant to share (per the backlog's own cross-reference note). Given a quantity/unit and a target system ("metric" | "imperial" | "weight"), it picks the "nicest" unit via a simple magnitude heuristic -- the largest unit in that system's own ladder that keeps the rendered quantity >= 1 (e.g. 4 cups under the imperial ladder renders as "2 pt", not "0.03 gal") -- NOT full culinary fraction-rounding (no snapping to 1/4 cup), a documented, deliberate scope cut. "Weight" mode always targets grams/kg regardless of the Imperial/Metric choice, matching how a kitchen scale is actually used in GF baking (the entire point of the mode, per the original backlog text). Count-based units (`"2 cloves"`, `"1 can"`) always pass through unchanged -- a legitimate state, never treated as a failure.

**Density-sourcing, the piece B5.3 explicitly deferred**: `food_data_service._parse_usda_density()` scans a resolved food's USDA `foodPortions` list (fetched via the same `get_usda_food` detail call already made for nutrients) for the first portion reporting a recognized volume unit (cup, tbsp, etc.) and derives an implied density (g/mL) from its `gramWeight`. Verified USDA's actual `foodPortions` JSON shape live (searched rather than assumed, to avoid silently building a parser against a guessed schema) -- `amount`/`gramWeight`/`measureUnit.name`/`modifier` fields, as documented. Open Food Facts does NOT get a density in this pass (no consistently structured volume-portion data to parse there) -- a stated limitation, not an oversight. New nullable `RecipeIngredient.density_g_per_ml` column (migration `d4f9c2a8e1b3`), populated by `resolve_and_cache_ingredient` alongside the existing nutrition cache, at the same resolution pass -- no new network call, no new user action needed beyond the existing "resolve"/"compute nutrition" buttons.

**A real, positive side effect on B1.2**: `food_data_service.compute_ingredient_grams()` now passes the ingredient's own cached density into `unit_conversion_service.convert()`, so a volume-measured ingredient with a known density (e.g. "2 cup flour" resolved against a USDA food reporting a cup portion) can now contribute to `Recipe.nutrition`'s computed sum -- previously only mass-unit ingredients ever could, meaning a recipe written entirely in cups/tablespoons would always land on "ai_estimated" even after every ingredient resolved successfully. This wasn't the point of B10.5, but it was a direct, low-risk unlock once density existed at all, and is documented as such in both modules' docstrings rather than left as an unexplained side effect.

**`GET /api/recipes/{id}` gained a `unit_system` query param** ("original" | "metric" | "imperial" | "weight", default "original"), applied after servings scaling, never touching the persisted recipe (per the backlog's explicit "not a stored per-recipe property, not a destructive conversion" requirement). `RecipeIngredientRead` gained `display_unavailable: bool` -- true only when weight mode was requested for a volume-quantity ingredient with no cached density; the ingredient's original quantity/unit are left as-is in that case rather than guessed. A byproduct of unifying this into one pipeline: `_to_read()`'s two previously-separate ingredient-rendering branches (scaled vs. unscaled) were consolidated into one path that always flows through `scale_ingredients` -> `apply_display_unit_system`, which also means `RecipeIngredientRead.id` is now populated even when servings are scaled (previously null in that branch, an implementation artifact rather than a deliberate contract -- corrected and re-documented, not a behavior anyone was relying on per a grep of the frontend).

**New `default_unit_system` DB-backed setting** (SettingSpec, default `"original"`), following the exact "instant apply, persist as the new default" UX the Appearance theme picker established this session -- changing the selector on a recipe page updates the current view immediately and PATCHes the setting in the background, so the next recipe view (or next session) starts from the last choice.

**Frontend**: `RecipeDetailPage.jsx` gained a "Units" selector next to the existing Servings control, seeded from `default_unit_system` on load; ingredients flagged `display_unavailable` get an inline "not available in {system}" tag plus a hint pointing at the "Compute from ingredients" button (which resolves-if-needed as a side effect, per B1.2, so it's a real fix path, not a dead end).

**Deliberately not done**: culinary fraction-rounding (1/4 cup, 1/3 cup, etc.) -- decimal quantities only, a documented scope cut, not a bug; Open Food Facts density; per-ingredient manual density override (a user-entered density for an ingredient USDA/OFF can't resolve or has no volume portion for) -- worth a small follow-up if real usage shows this gap matters, but not requested and not built speculatively.

**Verification**: 31 new unit tests (153 total in the repo, all passing) covering `_parse_usda_density`'s portion-matching and edge cases, `resolve_ingredient_name`/`resolve_and_cache_ingredient`'s density wiring, `compute_ingredient_grams`'s newly-enabled volume-with-density path, every `convert_for_display` branch (count passthrough, metric/imperial ladders at threshold boundaries, weight mode with and without density, unknown system), and `apply_display_unit_system`'s dict-transform contract (density-key stripping, unavailable-flagging, null-quantity passthrough, key preservation). Live end-to-end pass against a freshly migrated+seeded database and a real local uvicorn instance: a 4-ingredient recipe (a cup-of-flour with a manually-set density simulating a completed USDA resolution -- no live USDA network reaches the sandbox, same standing constraint as every prior B1.1-adjacent verification -- a lb-of-butter, a tsp-of-salt with no density, and a count-based "2 eggs") correctly rendered under original/metric/weight, including salt correctly flagged `display_unavailable` under weight mode while butter (already mass) and flour (density available) converted correctly and eggs passed through unchanged; confirmed servings-scaling and unit-system conversion compose correctly together (`unit_system=imperial&servings=16`); confirmed `default_unit_system` persists via the standard settings PATCH. Frontend `npm run build` succeeds cleanly (53 modules, built from a `/tmp` scratch copy per the standing FUSE-mount note). **Not independently verified**: on-screen appearance of the new selector/tags (no browser available, same standing limitation as every other UI-only change this project); no live USDA network access exists in this sandbox, so `_parse_usda_density`'s behavior against a REAL food's `foodPortions` payload (as opposed to the schema-accurate fixture used in tests) has not been observed firsthand -- worth a spot check against a real ingredient once the author has a USDA key configured and can view a recipe in weight mode.

## B4.2 notes: receipt/list bulk inventory import (2026-08-01)

Author-requested directly (not picked from the backlog top) after noticing the Inventory page only offered one-at-a-time manual entry or a single pantry-snapshot photo -- wanted a convenient way to bring in "volumes of items" from a receipt photo, a receipt PDF, or a text/file list. This substantially completes B4.2 but generalizes it beyond photo-OCR-only, so it's marked done there with a note pointing back here.

**Deliberately kept as a second, separate intake source rather than merged into `/vision-intake`.** The two answer different questions -- "what's currently in the pantry/fridge" (a snapshot) vs. "what did I just buy" (a purchase record) -- and need different prompts: a pantry photo just names visible items, while a receipt has to skip subtotal/tax/tender/coupon/store-header lines and expand POS abbreviations ("GV 2% MLK GAL" -> "Great Value 2% milk, 1 gallon") without inventing a specific brand/variety it isn't confident about. Both land in the same preview-then-confirm shape (`VisionDetectedItem`/`InventoryItemCreate`) since "one detected item before the user reviews and confirms it" means the same thing regardless of source -- no duplicated schema.

**Backend**: new `POST /api/inventory/import` (`backend/app/routers/inventory.py`) accepts exactly one of an uploaded `file` or pasted `text`, dispatching on content type/filename: an image goes to `ollama_client.describe_image` with the new `RECEIPT_IMPORT_PROMPT`; a PDF goes through `recipe_service.extract_pdf_text` then a chat-based extraction pass (`_receipt_text_extraction`, mirroring `recipes.py`'s own text-extraction helper); anything else is decoded as UTF-8 text and chat-extracted the same way; bare pasted `text` skips straight to chat extraction. A deliberate improvement over the recipe-import precedent: an empty-after-extraction PDF (a scanned receipt with no text layer, a common real failure mode for phone "scan to PDF" apps) fails with a clear 400 telling the user to upload it as a photo instead, rather than silently sending near-nothing to the model. New `POST /api/inventory/import/confirm` bulk-creates rows from the user-reviewed list, sharing a new `_bulk_create_items()` helper with the existing `/vision-intake/confirm` (refactored to use it too, no behavior change there). New response/request schemas (`InventoryImportResponse` with a `source_type` of "photo"/"pdf"/"text" for provenance, `InventoryImportConfirmRequest`) in `schemas/inventory.py`; `InventoryItemCreate.source` gained `import_photo`/`import_pdf`/`import_text` as documented enum values.

**Frontend**: `InventoryPage.jsx` gained a new "Import receipt/list" toggle button alongside the existing "Add from photo," opening a card with a text area (paste a list) and a file upload (photo/PDF/text file). Unlike the existing vision-intake preview, which is a read-only list, the new preview is a genuinely EDITABLE table -- each detected row has inline-editable name/category/quantity/unit/expiration-date fields, an include/exclude checkbox, and a remove button, plus any `confidence_note` shown inline under the name -- since a receipt can produce many lines and POS-abbreviation guesses that are meaningfully more likely to need correction than a single pantry photo's few items. Confirming posts only the included, as-edited rows.

**Deliberately not done**: OCR/structured price extraction (the backlog text's "populates inventory automatically with quantities and prices" framing) -- prices aren't inventory fields today (see B6.1), so this pass extracts name/quantity/unit/category/expiration only, same fields the existing vision-intake already captures; a receipt-derived price-per-unit is a natural follow-up once B6.1 exists. No dedicated OCR engine -- reuses the existing Ollama vision/chat path exactly like every other AI-assisted feature in this app, rather than adding a new dependency (e.g. Tesseract) for a task the vision model already handles.

**Verification**: 4 new unit tests (157 total in the repo, all passing) covering `RECEIPT_IMPORT_PROMPT`'s `.format()` safety for both the text and image-placeholder substitution paths, and `inventory_service.parse_vision_response` against a receipt-shaped fixture including a malformed/nameless entry (proving the existing defensive parsing needs no changes for this new source). Live end-to-end pass against a freshly migrated database and a real local uvicorn instance covering all of: no-file-and-no-text (400), an undecodable binary upload (400), a blank/no-text-layer PDF (400, the specific scanned-PDF message), a plain-text list with Ollama unreachable (502, confirming the failure degrades gracefully rather than crashing -- no live Ollama network reaches this sandbox, the same standing constraint as every other AI-dependent feature this project), and the full `/import/confirm` -> `GET /api/inventory?search=` round trip confirming created rows persist correctly. Frontend `npm run build` succeeds cleanly (53 modules, no transform errors) -- built directly against the mounted `node_modules` after discovering a `cp -r` scratch copy silently dropped a symlinked dependency across the FUSE mount, a new instance of the already-documented FUSE coherence risk, worked around by building in place with `--outDir` pointed at `/tmp` instead of copying the whole tree. **Not independently verified**: on-screen appearance of the new import card/editable table (no browser available, same standing limitation as every other UI-only change this project); the actual AI extraction quality on a real receipt photo/PDF (no live Ollama network reaches this sandbox -- the prompt's abbreviation-expansion and non-item-line-skipping behavior is untested against a real model, only against hand-written fixtures).

## B10.3 notes: generic order-history CSV/XLSX import (2026-08-01)

Picked as the next item after B4.2 -- of the three remaining B10 (author-requested, higher-priority-weight) items, B10.1 (dining-out finder) and B10.2 (notifications/auth, needs reading Fiduciary first) both carry real design work and/or an external-network dependency this sandbox can't exercise live, while B10.3's own backlog text already named a concrete, low-risk recommended build from prior research: a generic CSV/XLSX importer, no AI, no live network call required at all.

**Re-verified rather than trusted from the backlog text**: searched live for Walmart order-history export options again this session (a present-day fact that could have changed) and additionally found the specific open-source "Walmart Invoice Exporter" browser extension's README on GitHub. Confirmed the original finding still holds -- Walmart still publishes no consumer purchase-history API and no built-in export button -- and confirmed the deeper reason a hardcoded "Walmart profile" would be irresponsible to ship: even this one specific extension's own README doesn't publish exact column header strings (only a prose feature list), and there are multiple competing extensions (OrderPro, Walmart Data Exporter, this one), each free to change its own layout at any time. Building a profile against a guessed schema would violate this app's own "never guess, verify or say so" discipline the same way a fabricated USDA density or an invented allergen match would.

**Design consequence**: no pre-built retailer profile ships at all. Instead, `order_import_service.py` is a fully generic tabular importer: `parse_tabular_file()` reads CSV (stdlib `csv`, `utf-8-sig`-aware for Excel-exported BOMs) or XLSX (`openpyxl`, new dependency -- preferring a sheet literally named "Items" when present, since the Walmart Invoice Exporter README describes exactly that two-sheet Orders+Items layout for its modern export format); `guess_column_mapping()` suggests a mapping via ordered keyword matching (name/quantity/price/unit/date, in that resolution order specifically so a "Unit Price" header lands on price_column rather than unit_column -- a real collision caught by a test written against that exact header text, not assumed); `apply_mapping()` turns the raw rows into the same `VisionDetectedItem` preview shape every other intake source in this app already uses, skipping rows with no usable name (blank/footer/subtotal lines) and defaulting an unparseable quantity to 1 or leaving an unparseable price/date as `None` -- always with a `confidence_note` explaining what happened, never a silent guess.

**New `OrderImportProfile` model** (migration `e7a1c9f4b2d6`) lets the user save their own confirmed column mapping under a name (e.g. "Walmart") after getting it right once, so the backlog's real ask -- not having to remap columns on every re-export -- is satisfied without a pre-verified schema. Full CRUD (`GET/POST /api/inventory/order-import/profiles`, `PATCH/DELETE /api/inventory/order-import/profiles/{id}`). The new `POST /api/inventory/order-import` endpoint accepts a file plus either explicit `*_column` form fields, a `profile_id`, or neither (falls back to the auto-guess) -- precedence is explicit-fields > saved profile > auto-guess -- and always returns the applied mapping alongside the parsed preview so the frontend can show what was used and let the user correct it.

**New `InventoryItem.unit_price`** (same migration) -- nullable, since most intake sources still have no price signal; populated today only by this importer (and manually editable, like every other field). `VisionDetectedItem` gained matching optional `unit_price`/`purchased_date` fields so the shared preview shape stays a strict superset across all three intake sources rather than forking. This is the concrete piece of B6.1 (cost tracking) the backlog predicted B10.3 would deliver "for free" -- the price DATA now exists; the cost-per-recipe/weekly-spend computation itself is still not built, noted on B6.1's own line.

**Frontend**: reused the exact `importItems` editable preview table B4.2 built, extended with Price and Purchased columns (both editable, both optional) -- per the backlog's explicit "same review screen, not a separate UI" instruction. A new "Import order history" card handles the one genuinely different step this source needs before a preview exists at all: upload a file, see its headers, adjust a 5-field column-mapping form (pre-filled with the server's best guess), re-parse on demand, and optionally save the mapping as a named profile (shown as a dropdown on the next import). `InventoryItemForm.jsx` and the main inventory table both gained a Price column for consistency with every other model field already being editable/visible there.

**Deliberately not done**: no pre-built Walmart (or any retailer) column-mapping profile, for the verified-schema reason above -- the user creates their own on first use, one-time cost. FoodKeeper-style shelf-life defaults from purchase date (B4.3) -- `purchased_date` is now captured and could feed it, but that's B4.3's own scope, not built here. Cost-per-recipe/weekly-spend display (the rest of B6.1) -- only the underlying price data exists now. Receipt-OCR (B4.2) price extraction -- not retroactively added to that already-shipped feature's AI prompt, since it wasn't asked for and would reopen a completed, committed item.

**Verification**: 17 new unit tests (174 total in the repo, all passing) covering column-mapping keyword resolution including the "Unit Price" collision case, CSV parsing (including BOM-stripping), XLSX parsing (including the "Items"-sheet preference and blank-row skipping), and every `apply_mapping` edge case (missing name, unmapped/unparseable quantity, unmapped/unparseable price with currency-symbol and thousands-separator stripping, unparseable/alternate-format dates). Live end-to-end pass against a freshly migrated database and a real local uvicorn instance covering: creating a mapping profile, listing profiles, a 409 on a duplicate profile name, an order-import with no mapping given (auto-guess applied, subtotal row correctly skipped), the same file re-imported using a saved profile (correctly returning a null price when the profile's saved column name doesn't match this particular file's actual header -- proving the app doesn't silently fall back to guessing when a saved mapping doesn't fit), an unsupported file type (400), the full `/import/confirm` -> `GET /api/inventory` round trip confirming price and purchased-date persist correctly, profile update, and profile delete; a second pass confirmed the XLSX path end to end including the "Items"-sheet preference against a real generated workbook. Frontend `npm run build` succeeds cleanly (53 modules, no transform errors). **Not independently verified**: on-screen appearance (no browser available, same standing limitation as every other UI-only change this project); behavior against a REAL Walmart (or other retailer) export file, since none was available to test against -- only synthetic CSV/XLSX fixtures matching the general shape research described. Worth a spot check once the author has a real export file on hand.

## B10.2 notes: lightweight auth gate + in-app expiration digest (2026-08-01)

Picked as the next item after B10.3, leaving B10.1 (dining-out finder) as the only remaining B10 item -- it needs real design work (a new MealPlanEntry type, a location/radius UI) and depends on external network services (OSM Overpass, Tavily) this sandbox can't reach live, unlike B10.2's two pieces, both of which were fully buildable and testable here.

**Read Fiduciary's actual `auth.py`/`notifications.py` first, per the backlog's own instruction, rather than assuming their shape.** Both are substantial: `auth.py` is argon2 password hashing, TOTP MFA with single-use backup codes, a recovery-key flow, admin/viewer roles, rate limiting, and a host-CLI recovery/wipe tool -- built to protect real brokerage credentials. `notifications.py` is a full VAPID Web Push + generic-SMTP dispatch system keyed off an audit-log event stream. Both solve real problems for a financial app with actual attack surface and a compliance driver behind it.

**Checked scope with the author directly before building anything (via AskUserQuestion), rather than silently deciding to descope a security feature.** Chef's own backlog text for the absorbed items (B9.4: "ideally add optional single-password gating"; B4.4: "in-app banner at minimum; optional email/webhook") already describes something much lighter than literal Fiduciary parity, and Chef has no financial data behind it at all -- but "mirror Fiduciary" (B10.2's own phrasing) could reasonably be read the other way, so this wasn't a call to make unilaterally. The author confirmed: lightweight single-password gate, not full multi-user/MFA parity.

**Auth, built new for Chef's simpler model** (`auth_service.py`, `routers/auth.py`): one shared password (argon2-hashed, `argon2-cffi` new dependency), no per-user accounts, no MFA, no recovery keys, no host-CLI tooling. Defaults OFF (`is_enabled()` false until a password is explicitly set) so every existing install keeps working unchanged. The login rate limiter (`rate_limited`/`record_attempt`, an in-process sliding window) IS ported near-verbatim from Fiduciary's own `auth.py` -- genuinely domain-neutral, cheap insurance regardless of scope. Session state is a signed cookie via Starlette's `SessionMiddleware` (ships with FastAPI already, no new dependency beyond its own `itsdangerous` requirement) rather than re-deriving Fiduciary's own `request.session` usage from scratch. A single centralized `auth_gate` ASGI middleware in `main.py` enforces the session on every `/api/*` route except `/api/auth/status` and `/api/auth/login` -- deliberately NOT a per-router dependency sprinkled onto individual endpoints, which is the exact mistake Fiduciary's own `auth.py` docstring says its predecessor made (a token checked on only 23 of 127 endpoints). The password hash and enabled flag live in the existing `AppSetting` table under key names deliberately kept OUT of `settings_service.SETTING_SPECS` -- otherwise the generic `PATCH /api/system/settings/{key}` endpoint could overwrite the hash or silently flip auth on/off outside the dedicated, current-password-checked endpoints in `routers/auth.py`; a unit test asserts `is_known_key()` refuses both keys directly, not just via a router-level check.

**A real, independent CORS bug found and fixed while wiring this in**: `main.py`'s CORS config was `allow_origins=["*"]` with `allow_credentials=True`. Verified live via curl (with an `Origin` header) that Starlette's `CORSMiddleware` echoes back the literal string `"*"` for ordinary (non-preflight) responses in that configuration even with credentials on -- an invalid combination browsers refuse to honor for any credentialed request (a response can't say "any origin" AND "trust my cookies" at once). This would have silently broken session cookies in Chef's actual production topology (frontend and backend on different ports, no reverse proxy yet) the moment auth was ever turned on, with no equivalent problem visible in `curl` testing alone (browsers enforce this; curl doesn't) -- exactly the kind of bug that's easy to ship undetected without a browser to check against. Fixed by switching to `allow_origin_regex=".*"`, which always echoes the SPECIFIC request Origin instead of a literal wildcard; re-verified live that the fix produces a correct, specific `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` header. `frontend/src/api.js`'s fetch wrapper also gained `credentials: "include"`, without which the browser would never send/receive the cookie at all regardless of the CORS fix.

**A real middleware-ordering bug found and fixed the same way**: the first `auth_gate` implementation raised `AssertionError: SessionMiddleware must be installed` on every gated request once auth was enabled -- caught immediately by the live curl pass, not left for a browser to discover later. Root cause: Starlette's middleware stack makes the LAST-`add_middleware`-call the OUTERMOST layer (its request-phase code runs FIRST), the opposite of what a naive top-to-bottom reading of the file suggests. Fixed by registering the `auth_gate` dispatch function's `@app.middleware("http")` decorator BEFORE calling `add_middleware(SessionMiddleware, ...)`, so `SessionMiddleware` becomes outermost and populates `request.session` before `auth_gate` ever reads it -- documented directly in `auth_gate`'s own docstring so a future edit doesn't reverse it back.

**Notifications, scoped to the required minimum**: `inventory_service.get_expiring_digest()` reuses `compute_urgency()`'s existing expiration-day buckets (already built for `/priority-suggestions`) but filters to ONLY the expiration-driven reasons, split into `expired`/`expiring_soon`. New `GET /api/inventory/expiring-digest` endpoint; new `ExpiringDigestBanner.jsx` mounted in `App.jsx` outside `<Routes>` (same "stays alive across navigation" placement as `ChatWidget`) so it's visible from every page -- the actual gap the backlog named ("only surfaces passively when the user opens the Inventory page"). Web Push (VAPID) and SMTP email -- Fiduciary's own delivery channels -- are deliberately NOT built: the backlog's own text calls both "optional" and the in-app banner "at minimum," and Fiduciary's notification stack solves a materially different problem (fan-out for discrete security events via an audit-log hook) than "periodically check inventory for what's expiring," so porting it wholesale wasn't a good architectural fit even setting aside the added dependency (`pywebpush`/`py-vapid`) and complexity cost. Worth a follow-up if real usage shows the in-app banner alone isn't enough.

**Frontend**: `SettingsPage.jsx` gained a "Security" card (enable/change/disable, each requiring the current password where one already exists, mirroring the backend's own confirm-before-change discipline); `App.jsx` checks `/api/auth/status` on mount and renders `LoginGate.jsx` in place of the whole app when enabled-but-not-authenticated, re-checking status on a successful login rather than managing any client-side token (the httponly cookie is the entire mechanism).

**Deliberately not done**: TOTP/MFA, backup codes, recovery keys, multi-user accounts/roles, host-CLI recovery tooling (all confirmed out of scope with the author directly); Web Push and SMTP email notifications (documented above); tightening CORS to a concrete origin list instead of a regex (blocked on the still-open "no reverse proxy yet" simplification -- tightening now would mean hardcoding a port that changes per deployment).

**Verification**: 23 new unit tests (188 total in the repo, all passing) covering every `auth_service.py` branch (default-disabled, set/verify/change/disable password, the settings-registry-exclusion guard, rate-limiting with independent per-IP buckets) and every `get_expiring_digest` branch (expired-vs-expiring-soon split, "expires today" counting as expired not soon, sort order, configurable window, empty inventory). Live end-to-end pass against a freshly migrated database and a real local uvicorn instance covering: the gate defaulting off and every route working unauthenticated; first-time `set-password` both enabling the gate AND authenticating the calling session; an unauthenticated request correctly getting 401 once enabled; the set-password session's cookie working; logout invalidating that cookie; wrong-password login rejected; correct-password login succeeding AND correctly receiving a specific (not wildcard) `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` header alongside the cookie when a cross-origin `Origin` header is present (the exact scenario the CORS fix above targets); the rate limiter tripping to 429 at the configured threshold; `disable` rejecting a wrong current password and succeeding with the right one; and the gate correctly returning to fully open once disabled. The expiring-digest endpoint was exercised against two seeded real rows (one expired, one expiring within the window) and returned the correct split. Frontend `npm run build` succeeds cleanly (55 modules, no transform errors). **Not independently verified**: on-screen appearance of the login screen, Security card, or the digest banner (no browser available, same standing limitation as every other UI-only change this project) -- the login-screen flow specifically would benefit from a real click-through once the author has a browser on hand, given how much of this feature's actual value is in the UX of getting locked out/back in correctly.

## B10.1 notes: dining-out finder (2026-08-01)

The last remaining B10 (author-requested) item -- picked up right after B10.2, closing out the whole group.

**Re-verified the OSM tag research live rather than trusting the backlog's prior note**: fetched `wiki.openstreetmap.org/wiki/Key:diet:*` and its dedicated `Key:diet:gluten_free` sub-page separately, since the two disagree on one point. The general `diet:*` page's own "possible tagging mistakes" section lists `diet:vegetarian=limited` as a mistake to avoid, while `diet:gluten_free`'s own page documents `limited` as a real (if "dubious/under discussion") value specific to that tag. `evaluate_restrictions()` handles all of `only`/`yes`/`limited`/`no`. The bigger finding: OSM's `diet:*` namespace covers dietary *patterns* (vegetarian/vegan/halal/kosher/gluten_free/dairy_free/etc.), not FDA-style individual-allergen avoidance -- of this app's 10-entry allergen taxonomy (`allergen_service.ALLERGEN_CHOICES`), only **gluten** (`diet:gluten_free`) and **milk** (`diet:dairy_free`) have any real OSM tag at all. The other 7 (eggs, fish, shellfish, tree nuts, peanuts, soybeans, sesame) have no coverage whatsoever -- this is a hard, structural gap in the data source itself, not a bug, and every search result surfaces it explicitly via `allergens_with_no_data_source` rather than silently treating "no tag" as "no allergen present."

**Safety framing, the backlog's own explicit requirement**: `evaluate_restrictions()` never returns anything resembling a "safe"/"unsafe" verdict. A missing tag is reported as `unknown`, not as an implicit all-clear; an allergen with no OSM key at all is reported as `no_data`; even a maximally favorable `diet:gluten_free=only` tag is paired with a caution that it's a crowd-sourced claim about general menu composition, not a guarantee against a specific kitchen's cross-contact practices on a given day. The caution message is more pointed when the household's own `gluten_observance_level` is `strict_no_cross_contact` (B3.2), reusing that existing field rather than inventing a parallel one.

**Backend** (`dining_service.py`, `schemas/dining.py`, `routers/dining.py`): `build_overpass_query()`/`parse_overpass_response()` are pure and independently tested; `search_nearby_restaurants()` is the one function that actually calls the Overpass API (`overpass-api.de`, free, keyless) and is therefore untestable live in this sandbox (same standing constraint as every other external network dependency this project has hit). `GET /api/dining/nearby?lat=&lon=&radius_km=` clamps the radius to 100m-20km, queries Overpass for `amenity=restaurant|cafe|fast_food` nodes/ways, and evaluates each result against the household's actual `restricted_allergens`/`gluten_observance_level` (`HouseholdPreferences`, the same B3.1/B3.2 fields) -- returns a 502 with the underlying error, never a fabricated empty-but-200 result, when Overpass can't be reached.

**Meal-plan integration reused existing machinery instead of adding new entry types**: read `meal_plan_service.py` and `routers/meal_plan.py` before building anything, and confirmed a recipe-less `MealPlanEntry` (`recipe_id=None`) already confirms without any inventory-deduction attempt and is already excluded from grocery-list aggregation and the nutrition summary (both loops skip on `entry.recipe is None`). So the backlog's "likely a new entry type" guess turned out to be unnecessary -- the only genuinely new thing needed was a purely descriptive `is_eating_out` boolean (migration `f3b8d0a6c1e4`) so the UI can render "eating out" instead of a blank/forgotten-looking cell, reusing the existing `PATCH /api/meal-plans/{plan_id}/entries/{entry_id}` endpoint rather than adding a new one.

**Two real serialization bugs found and fixed during live verification, neither caught by unit tests alone** (both `routers/meal_plan.py`): (1) `create_meal_plan` built each new `MealPlanEntry` by listing fields explicitly and had omitted `is_eating_out`, so an eating-out slot included at plan-creation time would silently save as `False` regardless of what was POSTed. (2) `_to_entry_read`, the read-side serializer every GET/PATCH response goes through, also omitted `is_eating_out` when constructing the response object -- meaning even after fixing (1), and even though the PATCH endpoint's generic `setattr` loop was writing the correct value to the database column the whole time, every API response still reported `false` because the field was never copied back out of the ORM object into the Pydantic response model. Neither bug was visible from reading the schema or model in isolation (both simply default to `False`, a plausible-looking value) -- both were only caught by an actual live create-then-fetch-then-PATCH-then-fetch round trip against a running server, not by unit tests over pure functions or by an isolated schema-construction check that only proved Pydantic itself could hold `True`.

**Frontend**: new `DiningPage.jsx` (nav: "Dining Out") with manual lat/lon inputs (always available) plus a "Use my location" browser-geolocation button that fails gracefully with a message pointing back at manual entry (this app is commonly served over plain HTTP on a LAN, where geolocation is typically blocked); a radius input; results rendered with distance in both km and mi, per-allergen tags using the same human-readable labels as the Health page's allergen editor (`GET /household/allergen-options`), and the caution message always shown, styled as a standing notice rather than a subtle hint. A "Send to meal plan" action lets a result be attached to an existing plan slot (PATCH with `is_eating_out: true`, `recipe_id: null`, and an appended note naming the restaurant). `MealPlanEntryRow.jsx` also gained a direct "Eating out instead" / "Undo eating out" toggle so a slot can be converted without leaving the Meal Plan page at all; selecting a recipe from the dropdown while a slot is marked eating-out clears the flag automatically.

**Deliberately not built**: Tavily enrichment (menu pages/recent reviews) -- the backlog's own text calls this an optional secondary source; the required, safety-relevant piece is the deterministic OSM check above, and it's a reasonable follow-up once real usage shows whether OSM-only is enough. Google Places/Yelp keys -- explicitly optional/user-configured in the backlog, not built this pass. Find Me Gluten Free integration -- confirmed (again) it has no public API, only scrapers, which this project doesn't do.

**Verification**: 14 new unit tests (202 total in the repo, all passing) covering `build_overpass_query`, `parse_overpass_response` (node vs. way/center coordinates, unnamed-element skipping, no-coordinate skipping, distance sorting, address formatting), and `evaluate_restrictions` (covered-allergen tag read, missing-tag-is-unknown-not-safe, uncovered-allergen no_data, the milk/dairy_free mapping, the no-restrictions generic caution, and an explicit assertion that no "safe" verdict ever appears in the return shape). The full migration chain applies cleanly through the new `f3b8d0a6c1e4` revision. Live end-to-end pass against a freshly migrated database and a real local uvicorn instance: `/api/dining/nearby` correctly returns 502 with the underlying DNS-resolution error (no live Overpass reaches this sandbox, so the error path -- not the happy path -- is what's actually exercised here); creating a plan with an `is_eating_out: true` entry included at creation time now correctly returns `true` (bug 1, above); PATCH-ing an existing entry to `is_eating_out: true` now correctly returns `true` on both the PATCH response and a subsequent GET (bug 2, above); grocery-list and nutrition-summary endpoints remain 200 and correctly continue to exclude the eating-out entry, confirming the "no new exclusion logic needed" finding held in practice. Clean frontend build (56 modules, no transform errors). **Not independently verified**: on-screen appearance of the Dining page, the meal-plan toggle, or the allergen-tag rendering (no browser available, same standing limitation as every other UI-only change this project); `search_nearby_restaurants`'s actual HTTP call against the real Overpass API, since no live network reaches this sandbox -- worth a spot check once the author has network access to test against a real location; behavior against a real, densely-tagged urban area's worth of results (only small hand-built fixtures were available to test `parse_overpass_response` against).

## B9.1 notes: GitHub Actions CI workflow (2026-08-01)

Picked as the next item after B10.1 closed out the entire author-requested B10 group -- no B10 items remained, so this session fell back to the B1-B9 backlog and picked the lowest-effort item with no external-network dependency and no remaining blocker. B9.1 was the obvious candidate: re-reading it revealed its own text was stale (see the corrected line above) and that it was already ~90% done from prior sessions' incidental test-writing -- the one genuinely missing piece, a CI workflow, is small, self-contained, and (unlike almost every other candidate in the B1-B9 list) needs no external API, no live network call, and no design decision to check with the author.

**New `.github/workflows/ci.yml`**: two independent jobs. `backend-tests` installs `backend/requirements.txt` plus a new `backend/requirements-dev.txt` (just `pytest==9.1.1`, pinned to the version this repo's suite has actually been run against -- kept out of the production `requirements.txt` so the shipped Docker image doesn't carry a test runner it never uses) on Python 3.12 (matching `backend/Dockerfile`'s base image, not this sandbox's own incidental 3.10 venv), then runs `python -m pytest -q` from `backend/`. `frontend-build` installs via `npm ci` (using the already-committed `package-lock.json`) on Node 20, then runs `npm run build`. Both jobs run on push and pull request against `main`. `backend/tests/conftest.py` already redirects `DATABASE_URL`/`SECRETS_KEY_FILE`/`SECRETS_KEYRING_FILE` to throwaway temp paths before any `app.*` module import, so the suite needs no real database, no Docker volume, and no Ollama/Tavily/USDA key to run in CI -- confirmed by re-reading that file rather than assuming it was CI-safe.

**Verification, and its real limits, stated plainly rather than glossed over**: the YAML parses correctly and the installed package versions in this sandbox's existing test venv match `backend/requirements.txt` line-for-line, and `python -m pytest -q` (the literal command the workflow runs) passes clean (202/202) against exactly that dependency set. What could NOT be verified end-to-end in this sandbox, and why: this sandbox's `pip` has no route to PyPI at all (confirmed live -- `pip install` against a brand-new venv fails on DNS resolution for every package, the same "no outbound network" constraint that's blocked Overpass/USDA/Ollama all session, just not previously known to also cover PyPI specifically), so a truly from-scratch `pip install -r requirements.txt -r requirements-dev.txt` could not be executed here, only reasoned about via the version-match check above. `npm`, by contrast, DOES have a working route to its registry in this sandbox (confirmed via `npm ping` and a real, successful `npm ci` reinstalling all 46 top-level packages) -- but a full fresh install of `node_modules` followed by a build could not be exercised here either, for an unrelated reason: this sandbox's FUSE-mounted view of the working folder blocks `unlink()` on existing `node_modules` files (the same coherence issue already on record for this project), so `rm -rf node_modules` silently no-ops file-by-file rather than actually clearing the directory. The repeated, successful `vite build` runs against the existing (untouched) `node_modules` this session are real evidence the build itself works, just not proof that `npm ci`'s dependency resolution from a clean slate produces an identical, working tree -- worth a first real CI run being watched rather than assumed correct.

## B5.4 notes: grocery list grouped by aisle/category (2026-08-01)

Picked as the next item after B9.1, alongside a quick pass over B9.6 (see below) -- with the entire B10 group closed out, this session fell back to the B1-B9 backlog and picked the lowest-effort remaining item with no external-network dependency and no author check-in needed; the backlog's own text already flagged it as "low effort, high daily-use payoff."

**Reused the existing six-value taxonomy rather than inventing a separate "aisle" concept**: `GroceryListItem.category`/`InventoryItem.category` already share `pantry`/`fridge`/`freezer`/`produce`/`spice`/`other`, and those buckets map reasonably well onto how a grocery store is actually laid out (produce section, dairy case, freezer aisle, spice aisle, dry-goods aisles). The backlog text's "Tandoor implements... per-store section ordering" is a bigger, store-specific feature than this pass builds -- a fixed, sensible shop-order (produce/fridge/freezer/spice/pantry/other) is what shipped, not a per-store customizable one; worth a follow-up if real use shows that matters.

**The real gap was that nothing ever populated `category` for an auto-generated grocery line at all** -- confirmed by reading `compute_grocery_list`/`_persist_grocery_list` before writing anything, rather than assuming the field was already wired since it existed in the schema. New `meal_plan_service.guess_grocery_category()`: a keyword-based guesser, deliberately held to a *lighter* standard than `allergen_service`'s matching (documented directly in the module comment) -- a wrong aisle guess is a minor, easily-corrected daily-use inconvenience, not a safety issue, so an inexhaustive keyword list with an honest `None` fallback (never a falsely-confident "other") is the right tradeoff here, unlike allergen matching where a miss has real consequences. Wired into `subtract_inventory`: a real `InventoryItem` name-match's own category always wins over the guess when one exists (the household's own classification is a better signal than a keyword list), confirmed live -- a "flour" grocery line correctly took the household's own `category: "other"` from a matching inventory row rather than the keyword guesser's "pantry". The manual "Add item" endpoint gained the same guess-when-left-blank behavior rather than leaving every hand-typed item permanently uncategorized, while still respecting an explicit category if the user picks one from the new dropdown.

**Frontend**: `GroceryListPanel.jsx` now groups the list into headed sections in shop-order, with an "Uncategorized" section for the (documented, expected) case where a keyword guess and an inventory match both come up empty; the manual-add form gained a category dropdown defaulting to "(guess aisle)".

**Verification**: 12 new unit tests (214 total in the repo, all passing) covering every keyword category, the documented "bell pepper" vs. bare "pepper" ambiguity the module comment calls out, the empty/no-match-returns-None cases, and both `subtract_inventory` branches (quantity-bearing and quantity-less) getting a category attached. Two pre-existing `test_unit_conversion_wiring.py` tests that asserted exact dict equality on `subtract_inventory`'s return shape were updated to include the new `category` key rather than silently left broken. Live end-to-end pass against a freshly migrated database and a real local uvicorn instance: created a recipe with six ingredients spanning every category bucket plus one deliberately unguessable one (saffron), confirmed the persisted grocery list's categories exactly matched expectations including saffron's honest `null`, confirmed an inventory-row category override (`flour` -> `"other"`) correctly beat the keyword guess, and confirmed manual-add both guesses when left blank and respects an explicit override. Clean frontend build (56 modules, no transform errors). **Not independently verified**: on-screen appearance of the grouped list or the new category dropdown (no browser available, same standing limitation as every other UI-only change this project).

## B11.1 notes: shared background job queue, everything unified (2026-08-01)

Ported the *architecture* (in-memory serial job registry, poll-based status endpoint, header badge) from the Fiduciary project's `portfolio-api/app.py`, adapted to Chef's stack: `backend/app/services/job_queue.py` is a new module, not a line-for-line port, since Chef's queue needed to be domain-generic across six unrelated endpoint kinds rather than Fiduciary's narrower use case. Deliberately **serial, not parallel**: the author's hardware (one local Ollama instance shared by both features) doesn't meaningfully parallelize two concurrent generations -- they'd split one GPU/VRAM budget, risking OOM and slowing both down -- so a single daemon worker thread pulls one `(job_id, fn)` at a time off a `queue.Queue`. Deliberately **in-memory, not a DB table**: job status is transient operational state, not durable business data; a job's actual result (an inventory row, a recipe, a chat message) is written through the exact same commit path every other write in the app already uses, and a job lost to a container restart has to be resubmitted regardless of what persistence strategy was chosen, since there's no realistic way to checkpoint "a receipt photo was 60% analyzed."

**Every AI-consuming endpoint now enqueues instead of blocking**, returning `202 {job_id, status, created}` immediately: `inventory.vision_intake`/`import_inventory`, `recipes.import_recipe`/`chat_about_recipe`, `chat.send_message`, `meal_plan.generate_meal_plan`. `knowledge.upload_knowledge_file` is the one exception to the "returns a job_id" pattern -- it keeps its original `KnowledgeFileRead`/201 response contract (the file row itself isn't gated on indexing) and just moves the reindex call into a fire-and-forget job, since the frontend already showed an "indexing..." tag driven by `chunk_count == 0` with no prior polling mechanism to disturb. The conversion pattern, applied consistently: fast synchronous pre-flight validation (input presence, 404 checks) stays on the request thread; everything else moves into a `_run()` closure that opens its own `SessionLocal()` rather than reusing the request-scoped `db` dependency, since a SQLAlchemy ORM object is bound to the session that loaded it and isn't safe to hand across threads -- anything the closure needs from the request's own session is snapshotted to a plain dict/Pydantic model before the closure is defined, never passed as a live ORM object. Errors that used to get distinct HTTP status codes now surface uniformly as job `status="error"` with a message, since that distinction only mattered for synchronous HTTP semantics these endpoints no longer have. Found and fixed a real, unrelated bug while doing this: `chat_about_recipe` had picked up a duplicate stacked `@router.post` decorator during the conversion (an editing slip, not a design choice) that would have registered the same route twice with two different response models -- caught by rereading the file before calling it done, not by a test.

**Dedup via `dedup_key`**: `enqueue()` coalesces a new submission into an existing queued/running job of the same key rather than starting a race -- used for chat (`dedup_key=f"chat:{session_id}"`) so two sends against the same growing session history can't run concurrently and interleave incorrectly. Live-verified: firing two chat sends back-to-back for the same session returns the identical `job_id` with `created: false` on the second.

**Frontend**: `useBackgroundJob(storageKey)` (new hook) persists an in-flight job's id to `localStorage`, polls `GET /api/jobs/{id}` every 1.5s, and -- the actual fix for the reported "page goes blank after tab-switching away and back" bug -- resumes polling automatically on mount if it finds a leftover job id, since the job keeps running server-side regardless of whether any page is watching it. Wired into every converted endpoint's corresponding UI: `InventoryPage.jsx` (vision intake + receipt/list import), `RecipesPage.jsx` (import) and `RecipeChat.jsx` (recipe-scoped chat, storageKey scoped per recipe id), `ChatWidget.jsx` (persistent chat, storageKey scoped per session id -- notable because this widget is mounted once at the `App.jsx` shell level and never unmounts on route changes, so it now survives both navigation AND a full page reload), and `MealPlanPage.jsx` (generation).
