A new `JobsBadge.jsx`, mounted the same "outside `<Routes>`" way as `ChatWidget`/`ExpiringDigestBanner`, polls `GET /api/jobs` every 2s and shows the running job's label plus an ETA derived from `_typical_seconds()` (the median duration of the last 5 completed jobs of the same kind, or an honest "no history yet" until one exists) -- this is the app-wide "something is happening" indicator the author's bug report asked for, visible from any page regardless of which page enqueued the work. New `@keyframes chef-spin`-backed `.busy-spinner`/`.jobs-badge-spinner` CSS classes in `theme.css`, using `color-mix`/`currentColor` so they render correctly across all ~12 shipped themes without per-theme overrides.

**Verification**: 9 new backend unit tests (`test_job_queue.py`, 223 total, all passing) exercising the queue directly against its real background thread with real `time.sleep()` synchronization rather than mocks, since real concurrency behavior was the point -- covering success, exception-capture, worker-survives-a-failed-job, strict serial (not concurrent) execution, both dedup branches, unknown-job 404 shape, and the progress/ETA computation. A live end-to-end pass against a freshly migrated database and a real local uvicorn instance (after discovering and fixing three more `/app`-hardcoded default paths that don't exist in this sandbox -- `DATABASE_URL`, `SECRETS_KEY_FILE`/`SECRETS_KEYRING_FILE`, `SESSION_SECRET_FILE`, all already env-overridable, just not previously exercised from a non-Docker environment) confirmed: a recipe import, a chat send, and a meal-plan generation submitted back-to-back all enqueue into the SAME shared queue and are visibly, correctly processed one at a time (`GET /api/jobs` mid-run showed one `running` + one `queued` + one already `error`-terminal, in FIFO order across three completely different endpoint kinds) -- this is the literal scenario from the author's bug report ("upload a receipt, then ask Chef chat a question, then go mess with recipes") and it now queues cleanly rather than any earlier request dying; the queue drained back to idle afterward with no stuck jobs. Every enqueued job in this sandbox terminates in `status: "error"` (`[Errno -3] Temporary failure in name resolution`) since no live Ollama reaches this sandbox -- sufficient to prove the plumbing (enqueue -> worker pickup -> error capture -> visibility) but not model output quality, an honestly-stated limitation shared with every other AI-endpoint verification in this project. Frontend `npm run build` clean across all six touched/new files. **Not verified**: on-screen appearance of the spinner/badge/resume-on-navigation behavior in an actual browser (no browser available, the standing limitation on every UI change in this project) and real Ollama generation quality through the new queue.

## B2.2 notes: HEI-2020-inspired diet-quality score (2026-08-01)

Picked, along with B5.1, as the two most substantial remaining backlog items after the author explicitly asked for "the top 2 most difficult or long running tasks" to be implemented unattended while stepping away for the evening -- both stay within the already-connected Chef folder and only needed web search/fetch for HEI-2020 methodology research, so no new permissions were required.

**The real, sourced standard, and the honest limits of what this app can score against it.** Fetched the actual USDA/HHS scoring-standards table live (`fns-prod.azureedge.us/sites/default/files/media/file/HEITableAge2Plus-508.pdf`) rather than working from memory of the methodology -- all 13 components' real point values and density thresholds are used verbatim. But two real, load-bearing gaps meant a literal HEI-2020 implementation was not honestly possible this pass: (1) Refined Grains and Added Sugars need data this app doesn't have and can't derive -- whole-vs-refined grain classification, and added-vs-total sugars (the latter already investigated and rejected as unbuildable back in B1.3). Rather than fake either component, both are excluded from the score entirely, with the total reported out of 80 points, not 100, and `unscored_components` naming exactly why. (2) The 9 adequacy components (fruits, vegetables, grains, dairy, protein) need USDA "cup-equivalent"/"oz-equivalent" food-pattern units, which come from the USDA Food Patterns Equivalents Database -- a full per-food crosswalk this app does not have and a real follow-on project of its own, not something to fake in the same pass as the rest of this feature. The substitute built instead: a keyword-based food-group classifier (`diet_quality_service.classify_food_group`, held to the same "informational, not safety-critical" standard as `guess_grocery_category`) combined with a single averaged reference gram-weight per food group's equivalent unit, applied to each ingredient's already-resolvable gram weight (`food_data_service.compute_ingredient_grams`, reused rather than reinvented). The Fatty Acids ratio component is approximated as (total fat minus saturated fat) versus saturated fat, since this app tracks total and saturated fat but not PUFA/MUFA separately. Every one of these approximations is stated in the API response's own `methodology` field, not just in code comments -- the frontend renders it verbatim rather than paraphrasing it into something that sounds more authoritative than it is, and nothing in this feature is ever labeled "your Healthy Eating Index score."

Found and fixed a real classifier bug caught by a failing test, not designed around up front: "whole wheat bread" matched both the whole-grain keyword ("whole wheat") and the generic refined-grain keyword ("bread"), double-counting toward both groups -- fixed by making whole-grain and refined-grain tags mutually exclusive (a whole-grain qualifier in the name wins).

New `backend/app/services/diet_quality_service.py` (`compute_diet_quality_score`, scored over the whole plan rather than per day, consistent with HEI's own multi-day-averaging methodology and reusing meal_plan_service.compute_nutrition_summary's "one serving per person per meal" convention rather than reinventing it), new `GET /api/meal-plans/{plan_id}/diet-quality-score` endpoint, new `DietQualityPanel.jsx` alongside `NutritionSummaryPanel` on `MealPlanPage.jsx` with a collapsible component breakdown. **Verified**: 18 new unit tests (244 total in the repo, all passing) covering the classifier's food-group matching and the mutual-exclusivity fix, both no-data and fully-computable scoring paths, moderation-component interpolation (hand-verified against the real 1.1/2.0 sodium standard), unscored-component reporting, and ingredient-quantity edge cases (no quantity, unconvertible unit); a live end-to-end pass against a real local uvicorn instance and a hand-built recipe with known ingredients confirmed the returned per-component values and point totals match hand calculation exactly (55/80 points, with vegetables/grains/protein/fatty-acids/sodium/saturated-fat all scoring correctly against the real thresholds, and fruits/dairy/seafood correctly at zero given the recipe had none); clean frontend build. **Not verified**: on-screen appearance (no browser available, the standing limitation on every UI-only change in this project), and `_parse_usda_density`-style accuracy against a real (not synthetic) recipe corpus, since this is inherently an approximation by design, not something a live network call would make more precise.

## B5.1 notes: explicit leftovers modeling (2026-08-01)

**Design, kept deliberately simple rather than building a "cook batch" grouping concept the backlog text didn't actually ask for.** A single nullable self-referential FK (`MealPlanEntry.leftover_of_entry_id`) set on the LATER entry, pointing back at the entry whose cook event it's drawing from -- the backlog's own example ("a Sunday cook can legitimately fill Monday lunch") describes exactly a two-entry relationship, and a chain of these (Tuesday lunch also leftover-of Sunday dinner) already covers "one cook event feeds several slots" without a grouping table. The household is expected to set the ORIGIN entry's `servings` to the combined total actually cooked (e.g. 6 servings for a 2-person household's Sunday dinner plus Monday lunch), not just that one slot's portion -- this is a convention stated in the model docstring, not enforced numerically, matching this app's existing preference for honest documentation over invented validation where the "correct" number depends on something only the household knows (how much they actually cooked).

**Two real consumers of the flag, both found by rereading the existing code before changing it, not assumed:** `meal_plan_service.compute_grocery_list` now skips a leftover entry's own ingredient contribution entirely (the origin's contribution, scaled to the combined servings, already covers it); `routers/meal_plan.py`'s `confirm_meal_plan_entry` now skips BOTH the allergen re-check and the inventory deduction for a leftover entry's confirm (the origin's own confirm already ran both against the same recipe) -- the entry still gets marked `is_confirmed=True` for tracking, it just doesn't touch inventory a second time. Nutrition summary (B1.4) and the new B2.2 diet-quality score are deliberately UNCHANGED -- a leftover lunch still has calories and still counts as a meal actually eaten, so per-meal nutrition counting stays exactly as it was; only the shopping/inventory side double-counted before this.

New migration `470cd22a681e` (SQLite batch-mode FK add, mirroring the recipe-variant self-link migration `349c7fd3ed4c`). Validation lives in `update_meal_plan_entry` (the actual mechanism for linking/unlinking, via the existing PATCH endpoint rather than a new one) rather than trusting the bare FK column, since SQLite doesn't enforce FK constraints by default in this setup (an existing, previously-documented tradeoff): rejects self-reference and rejects linking to an entry in a DIFFERENT plan. `_to_entry_read` explicitly names the new field in its manual constructor -- learned directly from B10.1's own documented bug (both `create_meal_plan` and this same function once silently omitted `is_eating_out`), so this field was added with that exact lesson in mind rather than repeating it. Frontend: `MealPlanEntryRow.jsx` gained a "mark as leftovers of" picker (candidates are any other entry in the plan with a recipe assigned that isn't itself already a leftover of something else, keeping the relationship two levels deep at most) and a "leftovers from ___" badge resolved client-side from the plan's already-loaded entry list, deliberately not a new nested backend shape for information the frontend already has in memory.

**Verified, with real, hand-checkable numbers, not just assertions in isolation:** 3 new unit tests (244 total in the repo, all passing) for the grocery-list skip logic against a real DB-backed `compute_grocery_list` call. A live end-to-end pass against a real local uvicorn instance and a hand-built two-entry plan (a 6-serving Sunday dinner, a 2-serving Monday lunch, both the same recipe) confirmed: the grocery list totaled 4x the base recipe (both entries counted) before linking, dropped to exactly 3x (only the origin, scaled to its own 6 servings) immediately after linking and regenerating; confirming the origin deducted exactly the expected 1200g of rice from a 5000g starting inventory; confirming the LEFTOVER entry afterward left inventory completely unchanged (still 3800g) instead of deducting a second time; and both validation rejections (self-reference, cross-plan reference) returned the expected 400s. Clean frontend build. **Not verified**: on-screen appearance of the new picker/badge (no browser available).

## B2.1 notes: bundled default nutritionist knowledge files (2026-08-01)

**Picked as the next backlog item** after B2.2/B5.1/B11.1 closed out -- the only remaining item in the highest-priority-after-B1 group (B2, diet-quality/guideline grounding), and the one that closes a genuine gap against the project brief's own "grounded in nutritionist knowledge" requirement: the corpus shipped empty, so external users started ungrounded by default. Unlike most prior backend sessions, this one had real live web access (search + fetch), which the isolated pytest/uvicorn sandbox used for day-to-day backend verification does not -- used it to pull current, real source content rather than working from training-data memory of what these documents say.

**Five bundled files**, all under `backend/app/data/default_knowledge/` (shipped in the repo, copied into live knowledge storage and registered as inactive `KnowledgeFile` rows at seed time, per the backlog's own "inactive-by-default, user toggles on" scoping): `dietary_guidelines_2025_2030.md`, `dash_eating_pattern.md`, `portfolio_diet_ldl_cholesterol.md`, `fda_major_food_allergens.md`, `niaid_food_allergy_diagnosis_management.md`.

**Licensing verified per document, per the backlog's own explicit requirement** -- not assumed: DGA, NHLBI/DASH, FDA/FALCPA-FASTER-Act, and the NIAID guidelines summary are each fetched live from their .gov source and are works of the U.S. federal government (17 U.S.C. SS 105), not subject to copyright domestically. The Portfolio diet has no single federal source (it's academic/clinical research, principally out of the University of Toronto), so that file is explicitly labeled as Chef's own original synthesis of the cited peer-reviewed sources rather than a reproduction of any one document.

**Deliberately NOT bundled: DRI numeric reference tables**, despite being named in the original backlog text. Investigated rather than assumed: DRI reports are produced by the National Academies of Sciences, Engineering, and Medicine (NASEM), a private nonprofit operating under a federal contract, not a federal agency itself -- NASEM's own reports are freely readable online but are not U.S. government works and are not automatically public domain, so "verify each" (the backlog's own instruction) meant this one specific document category didn't clear the bar. Where this app actually needs DRI-derived numbers, they already live as cited, sourced logic in `app/services/dri_service.py` (B1.4) rather than a redistributed table -- the `dietary_guidelines_2025_2030.md` file's own "what was deliberately not bundled" section documents this reasoning inline for anyone reading the corpus later. Mediterranean-pattern content (also named in the original backlog text) was likewise not bundled this pass in favor of the two higher-priority-for-this-household patterns (DASH, Portfolio) given the author's specific stated LDL-cholesterol goal -- a candidate for a future pass, not forgotten.

**Wiring** (`app/seed.py`): new `seed_default_knowledge_files(db)`, matching on filename so it never duplicates a row already present -- whether from a prior seed run or a household's own same-named upload -- called from the existing `seed()` alongside its other idempotent steps. Reuses `knowledge_service.save_file()`/`extract_text()` exactly as a normal upload would, so a bundled file is indistinguishable from a user upload once created (same storage convention, same lazy-indexing-on-first-use path once a household activates one -- deliberately does NOT eagerly embed at seed time, since `ensure_indexed`/`search_knowledge` already skip inactive files and index lazily, so a fresh `docker compose up` needs no live Ollama just to seed).

**Verified**: 4 new unit tests (248 total in the repo, all passing) covering every bundled file landing as an inactive row with real extracted content, idempotent re-seeding, and the same-filename-as-a-user-upload collision case leaving the user's own row untouched. A live end-to-end pass running the actual `python -m app.seed` entrypoint twice against a fresh temp SQLite DB and a redirected `KNOWLEDGE_FILES_DIR` confirmed all 5 rows created inactive with real content on the first run, nothing duplicated on the second, and the on-disk copies present with matching byte lengths. **Not verified**: on-screen appearance of the resulting Knowledge Files list (no browser available, the standing limitation on every UI-facing change in this project) -- though this item added no new UI, only data, so the existing Knowledge Files page (Phase 6/RAG upgrade) is exercised as-is.

## B2.3 notes: Portfolio-diet-aware LDL steering preset (2026-08-01)

**Picked as the next backlog item** immediately after B2.1 closed out the rest of the B2 group -- the last item in the highest-priority-after-B1 backlog group, and a direct, concrete answer to the household's own stated LDL-reduction goal, which previously only ever reached generation as free-text `goals` the model reinterpreted every run.

**Design.** New `HouseholdPreferences.dietary_pattern` (nullable string, migration `b7e2f4a9d3c8`), distinct from the existing free-text `goals` column the same way `restricted_allergens` is distinct from `dietary_restrictions` (B3.1): `goals` is interpretive prose, `dietary_pattern` selects a fixed, sourced guidance block from a new small server-driven registry, `app/services/dietary_pattern_service.py` (`DIETARY_PATTERNS`/`get_pattern_guidance`), mirroring `allergen_service.ALLERGEN_CHOICES`'s existing shape rather than inventing a new registry pattern. Only one preset is implemented -- `portfolio_ldl` -- naming the Portfolio diet's four components (viscous/soluble fiber, plant sterols, soy protein, tree nuts) with concrete example ingredients and an explicit "not every meal needs all four" guardrail against an unrealistic, repetitive plan. The registry's docstring records that DASH and Mediterranean (both named in the original backlog text) are future work the shape already supports, not forgotten -- Portfolio is what the household's own stated goal actually calls for, and this pattern's own knowledge-file grounding (`portfolio_diet_ldl_cholesterol.md`, B2.1, written the same session) is meant to be read together with this preset's generation guidance, not duplicated inside it.

**Wiring.** `meal_plan_service.gather_generation_context` resolves `household.dietary_pattern` through `dietary_pattern_service.get_pattern_guidance()` into a new `dietary_pattern_guidance` context key (None when no preset is selected, the common case); `build_generation_prompt` renders it as a new `{dietary_pattern_section}` block in `MEAL_PLAN_PROMPT_TEMPLATE`, immediately after the existing household/goals/indulgence-frequency paragraph, empty string (nothing rendered) when no preset is active. API: `HouseholdPreferencesUpdate`/`Read` gained the field with the same "validate against the registry, reject an unrecognized key at the API boundary" discipline `restricted_allergens`/`gluten_observance_level` already established; new `GET /api/household/dietary-pattern-options` mirrors the existing `/allergen-options` endpoint so the frontend dropdown can't drift from the backend registry. Frontend: a new "Dietary pattern" select on `HealthPage.jsx`'s household-preferences form, right below Goals, showing the selected preset's description as a hint -- same fetch-options-once-on-load pattern the allergen checkboxes already use.

**Scope deliberately limited to meal-plan generation** (where the backlog text's own "concretely biases ingredient selection" phrasing points -- that's where a week's actual ingredients get chosen), not extended to recipe-scoped chat or recipe import/modify prompts this pass. A candidate for a later pass if the household finds itself relying on chat-driven recipe changes as much as generation.

**Verified**: 10 new backend unit tests (258 total in the repo, all passing) -- the registry/guidance-lookup pure functions, the prompt builder actually including or omitting the guidance block by literal string content (not just a context-dict assertion), and `gather_generation_context` correctly resolving no-row/no-pattern/portfolio-selected cases against a real DB session. A live end-to-end pass: fresh temp DB, full `alembic upgrade head` migration chain applying cleanly through the new `b7e2f4a9d3c8` revision, `python -m app.seed`, then a real `TestClient` round trip covering `GET /dietary-pattern-options`, `GET`/`PATCH /preferences` persisting and clearing `dietary_pattern`, and a 422 on an unrecognized pattern key. Clean frontend build (`vite build` via the established `/tmp` scratch-directory workaround for the FUSE `dist/`-cleanup `EPERM`). **Not verified**: on-screen appearance of the new dropdown (no browser available, the standing limitation on every UI-facing change in this project), and real model behavior under the new guidance block (no live Ollama reaches this sandbox) -- the guidance text reaching the literal prompt is verified; whether a real model's generated plan actually honors it is not.

## B5.5 notes: pantry-staple exclusion from the grocery list (2026-08-01)

**Picked as the next item** per the author's "proceed to the next item on the list," right after B2.3 closed out the B2 group -- the lowest-effort remaining item with no external-network dependency, same selection discipline as B5.4/B9.1/B10.6 earlier in this project.

**Design.** New `HouseholdPreferences.pantry_staples` (JSON list of free-text strings, migration `a1c5f8e2b4d7`), deliberately NOT a fixed taxonomy like `restricted_allergens` -- this is a household's own arbitrary "I always have this" list, not a checkable safety property, so free text with simple case-insensitive substring matching (new `meal_plan_service.is_pantry_staple`, matching either direction -- "salt" matches "kosher salt" and "oil" matches "olive oil") is the right rigor level, the same tradeoff already made for `guess_grocery_category`'s keyword lists (B5.4).

**Wiring, and why it's a stronger exclusion than the pre-existing quantity-less omission.** The backlog text's own framing ("aggregation currently only omits a quantity-less ingredient... when something matching exists in inventory") describes a narrower, already-existing behavior. A household explicitly declaring a staple is making a stronger claim than "there happens to be some in inventory right now" -- so `subtract_inventory` now checks `is_pantry_staple` FIRST, before any quantity/inventory-match logic runs at all, and skips the ingredient entirely when matched: quantity or not, inventory match or not. `subtract_inventory` gained a new `pantry_staples: list[str] | None = None` parameter defaulting to the old behavior exactly, so every pre-existing caller (`test_unit_conversion_wiring.py`, etc.) is unaffected; `compute_grocery_list` fetches the household's list via the already-existing `get_household_preferences` and passes it through. No new API endpoint needed -- `pantry_staples` rides the existing `GET`/`PATCH /household/preferences` the same way `dietary_restrictions` already does. Frontend: a new comma-separated input on `HealthPage.jsx`, right below Goals, matching the existing dietary-restrictions input's exact pattern.

**Deliberately NOT applied to manual grocery-list additions** (the existing `POST /{plan_id}/grocery-list` endpoint) -- a manual add is an explicit, one-off user action naming something they specifically want on this week's list, and silently dropping it because it happens to match their own staple list would be a surprising, unwanted override of clear user intent. The exclusion only applies to auto-generated lines from `compute_grocery_list`.

**Verified**: 12 new unit tests (270 total in the repo, all passing) -- the matcher's exact/substring/case-insensitivity/blank-entry behavior, `subtract_inventory` excluding a staple even when it has a stated quantity and even when inventory would otherwise leave a real remainder to buy, the `pantry_staples=None` default preserving prior behavior exactly, and `compute_grocery_list` correctly reading a real `HouseholdPreferences` row end to end. A live end-to-end pass: fresh temp DB, full migration chain through the new `a1c5f8e2b4d7` revision, seed, then a real `TestClient` pass -- `PATCH`/`GET /household/preferences` persisting `["salt", "olive oil"]`, a real recipe (chicken breast + salt + olive oil) attached to a real meal plan, and `POST /{plan_id}/grocery-list/regenerate` against the actual live endpoint returning only `chicken breast`, both staples correctly absent despite each having a real stated quantity. Clean frontend build. **Not verified**: on-screen appearance (no browser available, the standing limitation on every UI-facing change in this project).

## B6.1 notes: cost per recipe/serving and projected grocery spend (2026-08-01)

**Picked as the next item** after B5.5, per the author's "proceed to the next," since `InventoryItem.unit_price` (B10.3) already existed and was explicitly called out as "partially unblocked" -- the price data this item needs was already capturable, only the computation and display were missing.

**Design, live not persisted.** New `app/services/cost_service.py`, deliberately NOT a `Recipe`/`GroceryListItem` column the way `Recipe.nutrition` is: a grocery price is inherently time-varying (today's chicken-breast price isn't a fixed property of a recipe the way its nutrient content roughly is), so caching it would go stale in a way `nutrition_provenance` doesn't. `GET /api/recipes/{id}/cost` and `GET /api/meal-plans/{plan_id}/grocery-list/cost` both compute live, on every call, from whatever `InventoryItem.unit_price` data currently exists.

**Getting the price semantics right mattered.** `InventoryItem.unit_price` is documented on the model as "price paid for THIS ROW'S WHOLE QUANTITY as purchased," not a normalized per-single-unit price -- read carefully before writing anything, since a naive `unit_price * needed_quantity` would have been silently wrong (e.g. $6 for 2 lb of chicken is $3/lb, not $6/lb). The real per-unit signal is `unit_price / quantity`, converted into the ingredient's requested unit via the existing `unit_conversion_service` (same convention `subtract_inventory`/`deduct_by_name` already use) when the two units differ. Matching reuses `inventory_service.find_by_name`'s exact-then-substring, case-insensitive convention, restricted to priced rows, preferring the most recently purchased priced match when a household bought the same thing more than once at different prices -- never averaged or invented.

**Honesty discipline, same shape as B1.2's nutrition provenance.** Every result reports `provenance` (`computed` / `partial` / `no_data`) plus `resolved_count`/`total_count` and a per-ingredient `lines` breakdown with a `note` explaining exactly why an unresolved line stayed unresolved (no priced match at all; matched but no stated quantity, e.g. "salt to taste"; matched but the two units aren't convertible without a density this app doesn't have). `cost_per_serving`/`total_cost` are `None` whenever anything is unresolved into that specific sum rather than silently averaging over fewer ingredients than the recipe actually has -- a real live-verification finding (below) is that this happens more often than expected in practice (a household's own priced purchase and a recipe's called-for unit frequently differ in a way this app can't bridge, e.g. a receipt priced in oz against a recipe written in tbsp with no density on file), which is exactly the kind of gap this honesty discipline exists to surface rather than paper over.

**Projected spend excludes already-purchased items** -- `compute_grocery_list_cost` only prices a plan's still-`is_purchased=False` grocery-list rows, since a purchased item is spent money, not a projection of what's left to buy.

**Frontend**: `RecipeDetailPage.jsx` gained an "Estimated cost" section (fetched separately from the recipe itself, once per recipe id, since cost doesn't change with the servings/unit-system display toggles) showing cost-per-serving and a batch total scaled to the currently-viewed serving count, with the same computed/partial/no-data badge language as the nutrition provenance badge; hidden entirely when `provenance` is `no_data` rather than showing an empty/zero cost that could read as "this is free." `GroceryListPanel.jsx` gained an "Estimated remaining spend" line, refetched alongside the list itself so checking an item off as purchased updates the projection.

**Verified**: 21 new backend unit tests (283 total in the repo, all passing) -- exact-unit and convertible-cross-unit line pricing (hand-verified arithmetic), an unconvertible-unit case correctly staying unresolved rather than comparing raw numbers, the quantity-less-ingredient case reporting a real unit cost but no line cost, most-recent-purchase-wins price selection, zero-quantity matched rows, and the full recipe/grocery-list rollups' computed/partial/no_data branches including an end-to-end pass through the real `compute_grocery_list`. A live end-to-end pass against a real `TestClient` and a hand-built recipe (one exact-unit-match ingredient, one convertible-but-mismatched-unit ingredient, one never-priced ingredient) confirmed the exact expected partial result and per-line notes, then a real `POST /grocery-list/regenerate` and `GET /grocery-list/cost` pass confirming the remaining-need total after partial inventory coverage matched hand calculation exactly ($5.40 for 1.8 lb remaining at $3/lb); both 404 cases (missing recipe, missing plan) confirmed. Clean frontend build. **Not verified**: on-screen appearance (no browser available, the standing limitation on every UI-facing change in this project).

## B9.5 notes: iCalendar export of the weekly meal plan (2026-08-01)

**Picked as the next item** after B6.1, per the author's "proceed with the next item" -- a self-contained, no-external-dependency backlog item, same selection pattern as B5.4/B5.5/B9.1/B10.6 earlier in this project.

**Hand-built RFC 5545, no new dependency.** New `app/services/calendar_export_service.py` generates the `.ics` text directly rather than adding `icalendar`/`ics` as a dependency -- the format is small and well-specified enough to get right with a plain string builder, consistent with this project's existing preference for stdlib-only implementations where reasonable (e.g. `TrendChart`'s inline-SVG charts). Implements real line folding (RFC 5545 SS3.1, content lines capped at 75 octets with a leading-space continuation, verified live against an intentionally long recipe title) and real TEXT-field escaping (backslash/semicolon/comma/newline) -- not skipped as edge cases, since a strict calendar client (Apple Calendar in particular) will reject or mis-render a feed that gets these wrong.

**Live-generated, not persisted** -- `GET /api/meal-plans/{plan_id}/calendar.ics` runs `build_ics` fresh on every request, same pattern as `cost_service.py`/`diet_quality_service.py`. This is what makes the URL a real subscribable feed rather than a one-time export: a calendar app that re-polls the URL later sees the plan's current state (entries added, recipes swapped, slots skipped), not a stale snapshot.

**Date/time mapping, and what's explicitly a known simplification.** `MealPlanEntry.day_of_week` (0=Monday..6=Sunday) is applied as an offset from `MealPlan.week_start_date` -- this assumes week_start_date IS a Monday, which nothing elsewhere in the app enforces today, so an atypical week_start_date shifts every entry's date rather than being silently corrected. Meal times are a fixed per-meal-type default (breakfast 08:00, lunch 12:00, dinner 18:00, snack 15:00, each a 45-minute block) since nothing in this app's schema captures a household's actual meal times -- stated plainly in the module docstring rather than presented as more precise than it is. Event times are deliberately "floating" (no UTC designator or TZID) so a 6pm dinner reads as 6pm in whatever calendar app displays it.

**Skips `is_skipped` entries** (nothing to attend); an `is_eating_out` entry always summarizes as "Eating out" regardless of whether a recipe happens to be attached; an entry with neither a recipe nor notes falls back to "{Meal type} (unplanned)" rather than an empty summary.

**Known limitation, documented rather than silently left as a gap**: if the optional B10.2 session-cookie auth gate is enabled, this endpoint sits behind `auth_gate` like every other `/api/*` route -- but a calendar app subscribing to a plain `.ics` URL has no way to complete a login flow or hold a session cookie. With auth enabled, this only works as a manual download from an already-authenticated browser tab, not a live-subscribed feed. A per-plan share token would fix this and is reasonable future work, not attempted in this pass.

**Frontend**: a "Calendar (.ics)" link on `MealPlanPage.jsx`'s plan header, next to Delete plan, pointing directly at the backend URL (a real download/subscribe target, not routed through the JSON `api` helper).

**Verified**: 9 new unit tests (292 total in the repo, all passing) covering VCALENDAR structure, day-of-week/meal-type-to-datetime mapping for all four meal types, `is_skipped` exclusion, `is_eating_out` overriding any attached recipe, the unplanned-slot fallback summary, TEXT-field escaping, real line folding on an artificially long field, and the DTSTAMP UTC format. A live end-to-end pass against a real `TestClient` and a hand-built plan (a Monday-dated week, one breakfast, one dinner with a comma in its title, one skipped entry, one eating-out entry) confirmed every date/time, the skipped entry's total absence, the eating-out entry correctly ignoring its own attached recipe, comma-escaping and line-folding both firing on the real long title text, and a 404 for a missing plan. Clean frontend build. **Not verified**: on-screen appearance, and real-world import into an actual calendar app (Google Calendar/Apple Calendar/Outlook) -- no browser or such an app reachable from this sandbox; the RFC 5545 structural correctness (required properties, folding, escaping) is verified directly instead.

## B12.1 notes: Google Calendar push sync, and the in-app WIKI

**Architecture**: one-way push (Chef -> Google), Chef stays the source of truth -- deliberately not bidirectional, which sidesteps conflict resolution entirely and avoids depending on Google webhooks (which need a publicly reachable HTTPS callback this app has no reason to assume exists on a home LAN). `google_calendar_service.py` does the whole thing with plain `httpx` REST calls against Google's real Calendar v3 API -- no new dependency (`httpx` was already in `requirements.txt`), consistent with this app's existing preference for a hand-built integration over a heavier SDK where the surface needed is small (same reasoning `calendar_export_service.py`, B9.5, already used for the `.ics` builder). Reuses that same module's `_entry_summary`/`_entry_description`/`_entry_event_start` helpers rather than re-deriving "what does this entry look like" a second time, so the `.ics` feed and the live Google sync always agree; the one real difference is that Google events carry a genuine `dateTime`+`timeZone` (new `household_timezone` setting, IANA name) since these sync across real devices, unlike the `.ics` feed's deliberately floating times.

**Auth flow**: standard OAuth 2.0 authorization-code grant against a **Web application**-type OAuth client (not the Desktop/loopback-PKCE style the Local AI stack's `workspace-mcp` uses) -- chosen specifically because Chef can be reached from several devices/addresses on a LAN, and only a Web-application client lets an explicit, stable redirect URI be registered regardless of which device initiates the "Connect" click. `access_type=offline&prompt=consent` on every authorize request guarantees a refresh token every time, not just on a household's very first-ever consent. The OAuth `state` parameter carries a one-time, server-held pointer (module-level `_PENDING_STATES` dict, 10-minute TTL, same "cheap in-memory, not durable business data" tradeoff `job_queue.py` already documents) back to whichever frontend origin actually clicked "Connect," so the callback can bounce the browser back to the right device rather than assuming a fixed address.

**Bring-your-own OAuth client**, same trust model as Tavily/USDA FDC -- Google gives no way for a self-hosted, unverified app to avoid this. `SETTING_SPECS` gained 8 new entries: 3 the household types in (client id/secret/redirect_uri) plus `household_timezone`, and 4 written automatically by the OAuth flow itself (refresh token, calendar id, account email, sync-enabled toggle) -- the Settings page's generic settings loop now explicitly excludes those 4 (`GOOGLE_CALENDAR_MANAGED_KEYS`) so they're only ever edited through the proper connect/status UI, not a raw text box that could paste garbage over automated state.

**Schema**: one new nullable column, `MealPlanEntry.google_event_id` (migration `c8e1a4f0d5b2`), tracking which Google event (if any) an entry currently corresponds to, so `push_entry` can tell "create" from "update" apart and a skip/delete can clean up the right event instead of orphaning it.

**Sync triggers**, wired into `routers/meal_plan.py` at the three points that can change what's on the calendar: `create_meal_plan` (push every non-skipped entry), `update_meal_plan_entry` (push that one entry -- note `MealPlanEntryUpdate` has no `day_of_week`/`is_skipped` fields, so a PATCH can change what an entry looks like but never move it to a different day or skip it, keeping the sync surface smaller than it could be), and `skip_meal_plan_entry` (delete the event, clear the id). `delete_meal_plan` captures every entry's event id BEFORE the SQL delete (they're gone from the DB afterward) and enqueues a cleanup job. Every trigger checks `google_calendar_service.is_sync_enabled(db)` on the request-scoped session BEFORE touching the job queue at all, so the overwhelmingly common case (sync never configured) costs one cheap settings read per mutation, not a queue round trip -- verified live: creating/patching/skipping entries with sync off enqueues zero jobs.

**Manual force-resync**: `POST /api/calendar/google/resync` re-pushes every entry across every meal plan in the database (troubleshooting/after-a-long-gap use), returns a `JobEnqueuedResponse` so the existing `JobsBadge` (B11.1) shows its progress for free -- no new frontend polling code needed. Turning sync ON via the toggle also auto-triggers a resync, on the theory that the moment someone flips it on is exactly the moment they want the calendar to actually match reality, not a second separate click away.

**The in-app WIKI** (`frontend/src/wikiContent.js` + `pages/WikiPage.jsx`) replaced the author's original "GitHub wiki" suggestion once research showed why: asked to connect the sibling Fiduciary project and look at its wiki for structure inspiration, and it turned out Fiduciary's "wiki" isn't GitHub-hosted at all -- it's an in-app, data-driven help tab (`W(id, category, title, def, read, use)` entries, searchable, deep-linkable from a "?" affordance). That pattern fits Chef's own stated distributability goal far better than a GitHub wiki would: it ships with `docker compose up` for every household that clones this repo, works with no network/GitHub reachable, and needs no separate publishing step (a GitHub wiki page can only be edited by Claude via browser automation each time, one page at a time, with no local diff/review). Told the author about this pivot rather than silently building the originally-named GitHub-wiki version. Ported the same shape (renamed `def/read/use` to a small typed-block `body` array -- `p`/`steps`/`note` -- rendered by a tiny inline `**bold**`/`` `code` `` parser, no new markdown-library dependency for a format this small). Seeded with 3 entries for now (What Chef is, Settings & secrets, and the full Google Calendar setup walkthrough) plus a route/nav link (`/wiki`) and a deep link from the Settings page's Google Calendar card (`#/wiki?entry=google-calendar-setup`) -- **deliberately not backfilled for the rest of the app yet**, real future work, not attempted exhaustively in this pass.

**The setup guide itself** was written against the actual current (verified via live web search, since Google Cloud Console's OAuth UI was significantly reorganized in 2025-2026 -- "OAuth consent screen" became "Google Auth Platform" with Branding/Audience/Data access/Clients tabs) console flow, not from possibly-stale training memory: exact tab names, the free/no-billing-required path, why **External** audience type is the only right choice for a personal app (Internal requires a Workspace org account), why test users must be added regardless of publish status (Calendar is a "sensitive" scope), and an explicit callout that the "Google hasn't verified this app" warning during consent is normal and expected to click through -- named as the single most likely point someone abandons the flow. Also documents the exact redirect-URI construction (`http://<address that reaches Chef's backend>:<BACKEND_PORT>/api/calendar/google/callback`) and why it has to be the LAN address, not `localhost`, if anyone will ever click "Connect" from a device other than the one running the backend.

**Verified**: 22 new unit tests for `google_calendar_service.py` (314 total in the repo, all passing, run in two balanced chunks per the sandbox's known ~45s timeout) covering config gating, the state round-trip (including one-time-use), token exchange side effects (refresh token stored, account email fetched, calendar created, sync auto-enabled), the create/update/stale-404-fallback branches of `push_entry`, `delete_event`'s best-effort 404 handling, `disconnect`'s cleanup (including blanking every entry's `google_event_id`), and `sync_entry`'s skip-vs-push branching -- all against faked `httpx` calls, no real network. Live end-to-end pass against a real running backend (migration chain applies cleanly through `c8e1a4f0d5b2`): status/authorize/sync-enabled/resync all correctly gate on configured/connected state (400s with a clear message when they shouldn't work yet), the built authorization URL contains the exact expected Google query parameters, creating a meal plan with sync enabled genuinely enqueues a `google_calendar_sync` job (confirmed it actually attempted a real outbound call -- it failed with a DNS resolution error against the sandbox's no-egress network, which is the correct proof the real code path ran, not a stub), and disabling sync first correctly results in zero jobs enqueued for the same PATCH. Clean frontend build (61 modules). **Not verified, and cannot be from this sandbox**: an actual OAuth consent-screen round trip against real Google infrastructure (no live network egress reaches this sandbox, and doing so would require the author's own real Google Cloud credentials) -- the mocked unit tests substitute for this at the HTTP-contract level, and the live pass verifies every piece of Chef's own wiring around that call, but the author's own first real connect attempt is the true end-to-end test. Also not verified: on-screen appearance of the new Settings card and WIKI page.

## B12.1 correction: the Google OAuth redirect URI must NOT be a raw LAN IP

The author hit this directly in Google Cloud Console: pasting the LAN address Chef is normally reached at (e.g. `http://10.11.24.21:8096/api/calendar/google/callback`, exactly what the original B12.1 WIKI guide suggested) into "Authorized redirect URIs" is rejected outright -- "Invalid Redirect: must end with a public top-level domain" / "must use a domain that is a valid top private domain." This is Google's own OAuth policy, not anything wrong with Chef's implementation, confirmed against Google's own current documented Redirect URI validation rules table (`developers.google.com/identity/protocols/oauth2/web-server`): "Hosts cannot be raw IP addresses. Localhost IP addresses are exempted from this rule" and "Redirect URIs must use the HTTPS scheme, not plain HTTP. Localhost URIs (including localhost IP address URIs) are exempt from this rule." A bare LAN IP satisfies neither exemption; `http://localhost:<port>` (or `127.0.0.1`) satisfies both, for any OAuth client type including Web application, plain HTTP, no certificate.

**The author also asked directly whether calendar sync could instead be made to run entirely from the backend, to sidestep this.** Worth stating plainly: it already does, and always has -- `google_calendar_service.push_entry`/`sync_entry`/`resync_all` run on the backend using a stored refresh token, with zero browser involvement, for every sync after the initial connect. The redirect URI only matters for the ONE-TIME OAuth consent step, which is unavoidably browser-based in OAuth 2.0's authorization-code grant -- Google's server has to redirect the user's own browser back to something Chef controls after the user grants consent on Google's own page; there's no way to do that server-to-server without asking for the user's Google password directly, which this project's own standing rule (never handle user credentials) already forbids. Investigated whether Google's OAuth 2.0 Device Authorization Grant (RFC 8628 -- no redirect URI at all, a code is entered on any device with a browser, TVs/limited-input-device client type) could eliminate the whole problem -- checked against Google's own current docs rather than assumed, and it does NOT support Calendar API scope: the "Allowed scopes" section explicitly lists only `openid`/`email`/`profile`, two narrow Drive scopes (`drive.appdata`, `drive.file`), and YouTube scopes. Ruled out for this feature specifically, though worth remembering as a genuinely simpler option if a future integration only needs one of those scopes.

**Fix, two real paths, both documented in a new WIKI entry rather than picking one silently:**

1. **Loopback default (`http://localhost:<port>`)** -- Chef's Settings page now auto-suggests this whenever the browser's own address is a raw IP (`SettingsPage.jsx`'s `isRawIpHost`/`suggestedRedirectUri`), instead of the browser's raw address. Zero extra setup, Google-sanctioned, but the one-time "Connect" click has to happen from a browser that reaches Chef's backend AS `localhost` -- sitting at the server machine itself, or an SSH tunnel from elsewhere (`ssh -L 8095:localhost:8095 user@server`) for that one click.
2. **A public-DNS-to-LAN-IP hostname** (sslip.io/nip.io-style, e.g. `http://chef.10-11-24-21.sslip.io:8095/...`) -- a real, public DNS name (satisfies Google's check) that resolves straight back to the LAN IP; the actual HTTP traffic still stays on the LAN, only a one-time DNS lookup touches the public internet, and that lookup isn't really a new requirement in practice -- the same browser already has to reach `accounts.google.com` to complete the consent screen. Lets any device on the LAN click Connect without a tunnel, at the cost of depending on a free third-party DNS service staying up. Requires a manual paste into both Google Cloud Console and Chef's Settings field -- the auto-suggest buttons only offer localhost or the browser's own (possibly-invalid) address.

Both settings-page "Use localhost" / "Use this browser's address" buttons are retained, with the latter now disabled (and explained via a title tooltip) when the browser's address is a raw IP, rather than silently offering a value Google will reject. `google_calendar_redirect_uri`'s setting description in `settings_service.py` was rewritten to state the constraint plainly. **Not verified**: an actual Google Cloud Console save with a real sslip.io-style URL (would need the author's own real project); the loopback path IS independently verified via Google's own documented exemption table, not a live console test either -- reasonable to treat as reliable given it's Google's own stated policy, but flagged honestly as not something this sandbox can click through.

## B3.3 notes: recall awareness (USDA FSIS + openFDA)

Two free, public, government recall data sources, queried together because neither alone covers the whole grocery aisle (verified via each agency's own current docs, not assumed): the **USDA FSIS Recall API** (`fsis.usda.gov/fsis/api/recall/v/1`, no key, `field_product_items_value` substring search) covers meat/poultry/processed-egg products, which are under USDA's jurisdiction rather than the FDA's; the **openFDA food enforcement API** (`api.fda.gov/food/enforcement.json`, no key required though an optional free one raises the rate limit, `product_description:"<name>"` quoted-phrase search) covers everything else FDA-regulated. Both endpoints, field names, and query syntax were confirmed by fetching each agency's actual current API documentation and a real live query during this feature's research (not carried over from training-data assumptions) -- including the non-obvious fact that openFDA returns **HTTP 404, not a 200 with an empty list**, when a search matches zero records, which `recall_service.py`'s existing `except (httpx.HTTPError, ValueError): return []` pattern (already used identically by `food_data_service.py` for USDA/OFF) already handles correctly without special-casing.

**Matching**: name-substring, the same "best effort, not a guarantee" heuristic this app already applies elsewhere (`recipe_service._parse_ingredient_line`, `meal_plan_service.is_pantry_staple`) -- a generic inventory name like "chicken breast" only matches a recall whose product description literally contains that text, so a brand-specific recall logged under a generic name can be missed. Stated plainly in the module docstring and worth remembering when presenting this to the user: never framed as an exhaustive safety guarantee.

**No real cron/scheduler exists anywhere in this app** (a plain FastAPI process, no scheduler dependency added for this or anything else), so the original backlog's "daily/weekly check" is approximated honestly rather than faked: `check_inventory_for_recalls` throttles itself to at most once per `RECALL_CHECK_INTERVAL_HOURS` (24h, a fixed documented constant, not yet a user setting), triggered either automatically (`GET /api/inventory/recalls`, called on every Inventory-page visit, enqueues a background job via the same `job_queue` every other AI/external-API feature already uses when a check is due) or explicitly ("Check for recalls now" buttons on both the Inventory page and the RecallBanner). A household that opens the app at least daily gets effectively daily freshness; one that doesn't, doesn't get checked until it's opened again -- a real, stated limitation, not silently passed off as a background timer.

**Persistence**: new `RecallAlert` table (source, external_id, matched_item_name, title, reason, classification, status, recall_date, states, summary, is_dismissed) and a singleton `RecallCheckState` row tracking `last_checked_at` (migration `d2a7c4f19b3e`). Alerts are deduplicated on `(source, external_id)` so a still-published recall doesn't reappear as a "new" match on every subsequent check, and dismissal is durable (a per-alert `is_dismissed` flag, not a client-side "hide until reload" the way a less safety-relevant notice might reasonably use) -- both deliberate: a food-safety alert shouldn't quietly reappear because a browser tab was closed and reopened, and shouldn't nag forever about something already acknowledged either.

**Surface**: `RecallBanner.jsx`, mounted app-wide in `App.jsx` next to `ExpiringDigestBanner` (same "the app reaches out" placement, same "say nothing when there's nothing to report" behavior), expandable to show each match's title/reason/date/status with a per-alert Dismiss. Since the banner disappears entirely when there's nothing active, a small page-scoped line on the Inventory page (mirroring where Google Calendar's "Force resync" lives on Settings rather than app-wide) shows the last-checked time and a manual "Check for recalls now" trigger even when everything's currently clean.

**Verified**: 22 new unit tests (399 total in the repo, all passing, run in balanced chunks per the sandbox's ~45s timeout) covering FSIS/openFDA response normalization against fixture payloads shaped like each API's real, verified response schema (including the openFDA 404-on-no-match case), name-deduplication (case-insensitive, confirmed catching and fixing a real bug in this same pass -- the first implementation only deduplicated by exact string match, not case-insensitively, despite the docstring claiming otherwise, caught by the test written against that exact claim), throttling (both the "recently checked, skip" and "stale, check again" branches), dedup-on-persist (a re-check doesn't duplicate an existing alert row), and dismiss (durable, 404 on an unknown id). A live pass against a real running backend confirmed the whole request/job/persistence pipeline end to end: `GET /recalls` on a never-checked household correctly reports `check_due: true` and enqueues a background job; that job completes with status `done` (not crashed) after its real `httpx.get` calls to `fsis.usda.gov`/`api.fda.gov` correctly failed (this sandbox has no outbound route to arbitrary internet hosts, the same documented constraint every other external-API service in this project already works around) and were caught exactly as designed, `items_checked: 2, new_alert_count: 0`; a follow-up `GET /recalls` then correctly reports `check_due: false` with a real `last_checked_at`; the manual force-check endpoint re-enqueues correctly; dismissing an unknown alert id returns 404. Clean frontend build (62 modules). **Not verified, and cannot be from this sandbox**: an actual match against a real, currently-published recall (the parsing/normalization logic is verified against real fixture data pulled from each API during research, but no live sandbox request to either government API has ever succeeded) -- the author's own first real check, once deployed with real internet access, is the true end-to-end test.

## B9.2 notes: one-click backup, and JSON-LD recipe export/re-import

**Backup** (`backend/app/services/backup_service.py`, `GET /api/system/backup` + `/backup/manifest`): a downloadable `.tar.gz` of everything Chef stores -- the SQLite database, `secrets.key`/`secrets_keyring.json` (needed to decrypt every encrypted setting), `session_secret.key`, and the `recipe_images/`/`knowledge/` directories. The database is captured via Python's stdlib `sqlite3.Connection.backup()` API rather than a raw file copy, specifically to get a consistent point-in-time snapshot even while the app is in normal live use (a raw copy risks a torn snapshot mid-write). Each optional piece is included only if it actually exists on disk, so a fresh install still produces a valid, small archive. Hand-built with `tarfile`/`sqlite3` (both stdlib), consistent with this project's existing preference for a small, well-understood implementation over a new dependency where the surface is this contained.

**Deliberately no in-app restore.** Restoring means overwriting a live database and key files out from under a running app -- real, destructive, and easy to get wrong with a one-click button. The safer path for a self-hosted single-household app -- stop the container, extract the archive into the `chef-data` Docker volume, restart -- is documented step-by-step in a new WIKI entry (`frontend/src/wikiContent.js`, "Backups: what's included, and how to restore one", new "Data" category) rather than built and left under-tested. That entry also states plainly that the backup archive is exactly as sensitive as a password export, since it contains both the encrypted secrets and the key that decrypts them together -- called out both there and directly on the Settings page's new Backup card.

**Recipe export** (`recipe_service.recipe_to_jsonld`): the reverse of B9.3's `extract_jsonld_recipe` -- renders a `Recipe` row as a schema.org Recipe JSON-LD document, deliberately the *same* format the URL importer already reads on the way in, not a Chef-specific shape. New endpoints `GET /api/recipes/{id}/export/jsonld` (single recipe, downloadable file) and `GET /api/recipes/export/jsonld` (every recipe as one `@graph` array). Ingredient quantity/unit/name/prep_note gets flattened to one free-text line the same way every JSON-LD-publishing recipe site's does (schema.org's `recipeIngredient` has no structured shape either), nutrition values get their schema.org unit suffix restored (`"420 calories"`, `"9 g"`, `"500 mg"`) for round-trip-safety and human readability, and `tips` -- which schema.org has no standard property for -- rides along as a namespaced `chef:tips` extension property rather than being silently dropped, safe for any other JSON-LD consumer to ignore.

**Real re-import round trip, not just an export.** Refactored `extract_jsonld_recipe`'s node-search/field-mapping core into a shared `_recipe_dict_from_jsonld_document()` helper, then added `extract_jsonld_recipe_from_json()` on top of it for a raw JSON document (as opposed to one embedded in an HTML `<script>` tag). Wired into `routers/recipes.py`'s file-upload import branch: a `.json`/`.ld+json` upload is checked for structured Recipe data FIRST -- the same jsonld-before-LLM preference B9.3 already applies to URL imports -- before falling through to the existing generic-text/LLM path. This is what makes "Export recipe (JSON-LD)" a genuine round trip through Chef's own normal **Import recipe** flow (new `source` value `import_file_jsonld`), not a one-way archive dump; `chef:tips` is read back on this path too, so tips survive an export-then-reimport specifically (real-world sites will never set that key, so this is safe for the general HTML path as well).

**Cooklang was assessed and deliberately deferred, not silently skipped.** True Cooklang embeds ingredients inline within instruction steps (`Cook @onion{1} until soft`), which Chef's separately-stored ingredients/instructions don't map onto without either fragile substring-matching against step text or a fabricated, misleading-looking format. JSON-LD schema.org export already covers this backlog item's stated goal (a portable, interoperable export format) with a real, verified round trip; Cooklang remains reasonable future work for plain-text-oriented users specifically, not attempted this pass.

**Verified**: 29 new unit tests (377 total in the repo, all passing, run in balanced chunks per the sandbox's known ~45s timeout) covering `_sqlite_path_from_url` across absolute/relative/non-sqlite/malformed URLs, a real `build_backup_archive()` call against the test suite's own real temp-file SQLite database (asserting the snapshot is a genuinely valid, independently-openable SQLite file with the right data in it, not just bytes with the right member name), missing-optional-file handling, `recipe_to_jsonld` field-by-field (durations, ingredient lines, nutrition units, tags/keywords, rating, tips, image URL passthrough), `extract_jsonld_recipe_from_json` against valid/malformed/no-Recipe-node inputs, and a full `recipe_to_jsonld` → `extract_jsonld_recipe_from_json` → `coerce_recipe_fields` round trip asserting the reimported recipe matches the original. A live pass against a real running backend (fresh DB, real migration chain, real seed) confirmed: `/system/backup/manifest` correctly reflects what's actually on disk (database/encrypted secrets/knowledge files, in that exact sequence as pieces got created), `/system/backup` downloads a real, valid `.tar.gz` with the expected members, `/recipes/{id}/export/jsonld` and `/recipes/export/jsonld` both produce well-formed schema.org JSON matching a real created recipe, and -- the most important check -- POSTing that exact exported file back to `/recipes/import` completed successfully (job status `done`, not `error`) with the reimported recipe's title/servings/prep-cook-time/instructions/ingredients/nutrition matching the original, all without any Ollama call (the response's `raw_model_output` correctly states Ollama wasn't used), so this path works even though this sandbox has no live Ollama to test the LLM-fallback paths against. Clean frontend build (61 modules). **Not verified**: on-screen appearance of the new Backup card and Export button, and a real restore following the new WIKI instructions against an actual Docker volume (this sandbox has no Docker Compose stack of its own to restore into).

## B12.1 follow-up notes: three author-reported UX fixes (2026-08-01, same day)

The author connected their own real Google Cloud OAuth client and reported three problems from a live Settings-page screenshot, all fixed in the same pass:

**1. "Connect Google Calendar" gave no visible feedback on click.** Root cause, found by tracing the actual React state lifecycle rather than guessing: `gcalStatus` (whose `configured` flag gates the Connect button's `disabled` prop) was fetched exactly once in a mount-only `useEffect` and never touched again -- so saving valid OAuth credentials through the generic settings loop never actually flipped the button live. The button stayed silently disabled and a click did nothing, exactly matching "no feedback." Fixed by calling a new `refreshGcalStatus()` from inside `saveSetting()` whenever one of the three OAuth config keys (`google_calendar_client_id`/`_client_secret`/`_redirect_uri`) saves successfully.

**2. No error surfaced even for a genuinely bad config.** Defense-in-depth on top of fix 1: `GET /api/calendar/google/authorize` changed from issuing a server-side `RedirectResponse` straight to Google to returning `{"authorize_url": ...}` as JSON. The frontend's `connectGoogleCalendar()` now fetches that first and only sets `window.location.href` on success; a 400 (not configured, etc.) is caught and shown inline via the same `gcalError` state every other action on the card already uses, instead of navigating the whole page away to an unstyled FastAPI JSON error blob. Button now shows "Connecting..." and disables itself while the fetch is in flight.

**3. Redirect URI required manually opening `.env` to find the backend port.** `frontend/src/api.js`'s existing `backendOrigin` constant already carries the correct, BACKEND_PORT-aware host for the current browser session (sourced from `window.__CHEF_CONFIG__.backendPort`, populated by `docker-entrypoint.sh` from the container's env var) -- reused directly rather than inventing a second mechanism. `applySettings()` now prefills the redirect URI field with `${backendOrigin}/api/calendar/google/callback` the first time it loads empty, and a new "Use this browser's address ({backendOrigin})" button next to the field lets it be re-filled on demand (useful if a different device will do the connecting). The WIKI's Google Calendar setup guide and the setting's own description text were both updated to describe this instead of manual `.env` lookup.

**Verified live** against a real running backend: `GET /authorize` with no client configured returns `400` with the same clear message the WIKI references; after PATCHing in client id/secret/redirect_uri, the same call returns `200` with a well-formed `authorize_url` containing the exact expected Google query parameters (`client_id`, `redirect_uri` matching what was just saved, `response_type=code`, the calendar scope, `access_type=offline&prompt=consent`, a fresh `state`). Clean frontend build (61 modules, 2.84s). **Not verified, and cannot be from this sandbox**: the actual on-screen button behavior in a real browser (busy-state styling, error message rendering) and a real end-to-end OAuth consent round trip with the author's live credentials -- the author's own next click-through remains the true test of fixes 1 and 2.

## B9.3 notes: structured (schema.org JSON-LD) recipe import

`routers/recipes.py`'s URL-import branch now tries `recipe_service.extract_jsonld_recipe(html)` first -- most recipe sites publish a machine-readable `Recipe` block in a `<script type="application/ld+json">` tag (mainly because Google requires it for rich-result eligibility, which is most of why it's so widespread) -- and only falls through to the existing Ollama extraction path (unchanged) when the page doesn't publish one, it's malformed, or the parseable node has no `name`. `extract_jsonld_recipe` never raises for "not found," only returns `None`, so the fallback is a clean, expected branch, not exception handling.

Uses `lxml.html` (already an indirect dependency -- trafilatura is built on it, confirmed importable, so no new package) to find the script tags rather than a hand-rolled regex over raw HTML, which breaks on nested braces/quotes/CDATA real markup produces. Handles the three JSON-LD document shapes actually seen in the wild: a single `Recipe` object, a list of objects (a `Recipe` alongside e.g. a `BreadcrumbList` sibling), and a `{"@graph": [...]}` wrapper (common with Yoast SEO on WordPress sites). `@type` as either a bare string or a list (`["Recipe", "NewsArticle"]`) is handled.

Field mapping: `recipeYield` (free text like "4 servings") -> `default_servings` via a leading-number extractor; `prepTime`/`cookTime` (real ISO 8601 durations, e.g. `"PT1H30M"`) -> minutes via a small regex parser (day/second components ignored -- irrelevant for recipe timing); `recipeInstructions` (a plain string, a list of strings, a list of `HowToStep` objects, or nested `HowToSection` objects) -> a flat `list[str]` via a small recursive flattener; `nutrition` (a `NutritionInformation` object) -> this app's nutrition dict via a fixed property-name map (`_JSONLD_NUTRITION_MAP`) -- units are safe to assume from the schema.org property definitions themselves (calories in kcal, protein/fat/carb/fiber/sugar in grams, sodium/cholesterol in milligrams), so a leading-number extraction is correct regardless of whether the source also wrote out a unit suffix.

**The genuinely hard part, stated plainly as a heuristic, not a guarantee**: `recipeIngredient` is itself just a list of free-text strings in the schema -- there's no structured quantity/unit even in well-formed JSON-LD. `_parse_ingredient_line` splits a leading quantity (integer, decimal, simple fraction, mixed number like "1 1/2", or a unicode fraction character like "½") from an optional following unit word (checked against `unit_conversion_service`'s real volume/mass registry first, then a small hand-curated count-descriptor map for words like "clove"/"can"/"large" that registry doesn't cover since it's scoped to real conversion, not text parsing), then splits the remainder on its first comma into ingredient name vs. a prep note (`"onion, diced"` -> name "onion", prep_note "diced"). This degrades gracefully rather than dangerously on the genuinely hard cases (e.g. `"1 (15 oz) can black beans, drained"` keeps the parenthetical embedded in the name rather than crashing or silently dropping data) -- acceptable because, like every other import path in this app, the result is a PREVIEW the user reviews/edits before anything saves, the same safety net the Ollama path already relies on.

Citation handling: JSON-LD's own `author`/`image` (when present) are preferred over trafilatura's page-level metadata guess, since a byline picked up from general page metadata can pick up a site editor rather than the actual recipe's author, and a JSON-LD `image` is far more likely to be the actual dish photo than an arbitrary og:image. Falls back to the pre-existing trafilatura-derived citation fields when JSON-LD doesn't supply them. New `source` value `import_url_jsonld` (alongside the existing `import_url`) so a saved recipe's provenance is distinguishable later if that's ever useful. `raw_model_output` on the response is set to a plain marker string noting Ollama wasn't used, rather than left awkwardly empty, since `RecipeImportResponse.raw_model_output` is a required non-optional field.

**Verified**: 34 new unit tests (348 total in the repo, all passing, run in balanced chunks per the sandbox's ~45s timeout) covering the quantity/unit/prep-note parser against realistic ingredient lines (plain units, plural mass units, count descriptors, mixed numbers, unicode fractions, no-leading-quantity lines like "Salt and pepper to taste"), ISO 8601 duration parsing, numeric extraction from free text, instruction flattening across all four real-world shapes, the nutrition property map, and `extract_jsonld_recipe` end-to-end over hand-built HTML fixtures covering a single object, an `@graph` wrapper, a sibling-array document, `@type` as a list, plain-string instructions, a nameless (unusable) Recipe node, and a page with no JSON-LD at all -- plus confirming the function's output round-trips cleanly through the SAME `coerce_recipe_fields()` the Ollama path already uses, exercising real integration rather than a parallel, untested shape. A live pass against a real running backend confirmed the app boots cleanly with the new code paths present and the existing text-import path is unaffected (same pre-existing "no live Ollama reachable from this sandbox" limitation on the job's own execution, not a regression). **Not verified, and explicitly noted as a real gap rather than skipped quietly**: a true end-to-end fetch of a real recipe website's actual HTML. This sandbox's own outbound network doesn't reach arbitrary sites (confirmed by a DNS resolution failure on an unrelated live check this same session), and the available web-fetch tool converts pages to readable text rather than exposing raw HTML/script tags, so it can't stand in either -- one attempt against a real recipe site returned nothing usable, a second was domain-blocked. The hand-built fixtures are deliberately modeled on real, common JSON-LD shapes (the WPRM/Tasty Recipes WordPress plugin pattern most food blogs use, Yoast SEO's `@graph` wrapping, and Google's own documented HowToStep/HowToSection nesting), but the author's own first real URL import is the true end-to-end test.

## B4.1 / B13.1 / B14.1 notes: barcode scanning, recipe folder import, and Settings reorganization (2026-08-01)

All three shipped together in the same round, following the author's "EUREKA!!! using 'localhost' worked" message confirming the B12.1 OAuth fix, and the author's own explicit request: "Proceed with the next phase in the project plan (barcode reading is it?), but include the following: -I have a onedrive folder pre-populated with recipes, I'd like to support a 'folder import' option. -I'd like the settings 'Connection Status' section to include a status for any integrations... -The Settings section is getting crowded, can we organize & group common settings in less monolithic manner? ... Be sure that barcode scanning is appropriate for smart phones, ipads, and desktops. I don't have a real barcode scanner peripheral."

**B4.1 -- barcode scanning.** Researched actual 2026 `BarcodeDetector` Web API browser support before building rather than assuming Chrome-only coverage was fine: confirmed live that Safari, and every browser on iOS/iPadOS (WebKit is the only rendering engine Apple allows there, so "Chrome for iOS"/"Firefox for iOS" inherit the same gap), do not implement it, with no Apple-announced timeline. Since the author explicitly needs phone/iPad support with no physical scanner, built the ENTIRE scanner on `@zxing/browser`/`@zxing/library` (pure-JS, in-browser decoding) rather than "native API with a JS fallback" -- one code path for every platform, not a second, less-tested path for iOS users. New `frontend/src/components/BarcodeScanner.jsx`: lists camera devices via `BrowserCodeReader.listVideoInputDevices()`, prefers a device labeled "back"/"rear"/"environment" (most phones/tablets label their rear camera this way), decodes continuously via `decodeFromVideoDevice`, and includes a manual barcode-number entry field for when a camera isn't available/practical (a laptop webcam can decode a standard UPC-A/EAN-13 at close range, but less reliably than a phone camera -- a real hardware limitation, not something code can fix) or a barcode is too worn to scan. Backend: `GET /api/inventory/barcode-lookup?barcode=X` (plain sync `def`, not a job_queue job -- one fast Open Food Facts HTTP round trip, same B11.1 discipline that only genuinely slow Ollama calls need a background job) reuses `food_data_service.get_off_product` + `meal_plan_service.guess_grocery_category`, returning a `BarcodeLookupResponse` preview (name/brand/package-size text/category/image, `found=False` when OFF has no match -- crowd-sourced, so local/store-brand items are often missing). New inventory `source` value `"barcode"`. No confirm endpoint needed (unlike batch intakes) since a scan is always exactly one item -- the frontend just POSTs a normal `InventoryItemCreate` to `POST /api/inventory` after review.

**B13.1 -- recipe folder import.** Scoped via `AskUserQuestion` (local folder mount vs. a second OAuth integration) -- author picked local folder mount, avoiding a second painful OAuth flow right after B12.1's redirect-URI friction, and this approach works for any sync client (OneDrive, Dropbox, Google Drive desktop), not just OneDrive. The real engineering work here was refactoring, not new logic: the JSON-LD/image/PDF/text branching that used to live entirely inline inside `routers/recipes.py`'s `import_recipe` (reachable only via a single browser upload) was pulled out into two shared `recipe_service` functions -- `parse_recipe_file_content()` (per-file extraction) and `finish_recipe_parse()` (the raw-output-or-jsonld -> final `RecipeCreate` dict tail step) -- so the new batch importer and the existing single-upload path can never silently drift apart; the single-upload endpoint itself is now three lines shorter and gained a genuinely new `.html`/`.htm` branch (tries the file's own schema.org JSON-LD first, same B9.3 preference) as a side effect, for free. New `recipe_folder_import_service.py`: `list_importable_files()` walks the configured folder recursively (skipping dotfiles/dot-directories, e.g. a sync client's own housekeeping folders), filtering to `.txt/.md/.pdf/.json/.jsonld/.html/.htm` (deliberately no bare image files -- batch-vision-parsing a folder of photos is a much heavier, slower operation than asked for), capped at 300 files / 5 MB per file so a misconfigured mount can't turn one click into an unbounded job. `scan_and_parse()` is the job body (runs via `job_queue`, same as every other Ollama-batch operation) and never lets one bad file sink the whole batch -- a per-file failure becomes that file's own `"error"` entry, not a fatal error. New endpoints `POST /api/recipes/import-folder/scan` (returns a job_id) and `POST /api/recipes/import-folder/confirm` (bulk-create from the reviewed subset, looped over the existing single-recipe create logic -- no bulk-INSERT primitive added for what's a low-frequency operation). New `recipe_import_folder_path` setting (a path INSIDE the container; the actual host folder is bind-mounted via a new commented example line in `docker-compose.yml`), `.env.example` entry, and a new "Recipe folder import" WIKI entry under Integrations walking through the OneDrive-desktop-sync-client + volume-mount + Settings steps end to end. Frontend: `RecipesPage.jsx` gained an "Import from folder" toggle, reusing the `useBackgroundJob` hook and a review table (include checkbox, editable title, ingredient count, per-row parse status/error) before confirming, matching every other batch-import UI in this app.

**B14.1 -- Settings reorganization.** New generic `integrations` list on `GET /api/system/status` (`{key, label, configured, connected, detail}` per integration -- `connected: None` means "not applicable," distinct from `connected: False` meaning "configured but not yet connected") so a future integration only needs one new entry, not a frontend schema change; covers Google Calendar and the new recipe-folder path today. `frontend/src/pages/SettingsPage.jsx` was restructured around a small `SETTINGS_TABS` array (Appearance / AI & Models / Integrations / Preferences / Security / Backup & Data) with the active tab persisted to `localStorage`, mirroring the sibling Fiduciary project's own `panelSubtabs()` pattern (read directly, not assumed, before building this). The generic settings loop (previously one long, undifferentiated card) is now bucketed by key into the right tab via a small `settingsTabFor()` lookup, sharing one `renderSettingRow()` helper across all three tabs rather than duplicating the per-row JSX three times. Connection status is deliberately NOT one of the tabs -- it's pinned above the tab bar, unconditionally visible regardless of which tab is open, per the author's own explicit ask, and now renders the new `integrations` array alongside the pre-existing Ollama/Tavily dots.

**Verified**: 418 backend tests passing (14 new: 5 for barcode-lookup mocking `food_data_service.httpx.get`, 11 for `recipe_folder_import_service`'s file-discovery/filtering and per-file success/error handling via `tmp_path` + monkeypatched `recipe_service` calls, 3 for the folder-import confirm bulk-create endpoint), run in balanced chunks per the sandbox's ~45s command timeout, including a full re-run of every pre-existing recipe-related test file to confirm the `parse_recipe_file_content`/`finish_recipe_parse` extraction caught no regression versus the original inline logic (it initially did -- a missing `citation["source_author"]` assignment in the JSON-LD branches, caught by re-reading the original code side-by-side rather than trusting the refactor by inspection alone, and fixed before landing). Clean frontend build (302 modules, up from 62 -- confirms `@zxing/browser`/`@zxing/library` bundled correctly; one pre-existing "chunk larger than 500kB" warning, a performance note, not an error, not addressed this pass). `@zxing/library`'s own `package.json` declares `"engines": {"node": ">= 24.0.0"}` against this repo's `node:22-slim` Docker images -- confirmed this is a non-enforced `npm warn EBADENGINE` only (no `.npmrc` `engine-strict` anywhere in the repo), not a real incompatibility; the package is pure JS with no Node-24-only runtime API used. **Not verified, and cannot be from this sandbox**: actual on-screen camera permission/scanning behavior on a real phone/iPad/desktop browser (this sandbox has no camera and no way to grant one), a real end-to-end OneDrive-folder scan against the author's actual recipe files, and visual appearance of the new Settings sub-tabs.

## B15.1 / B4.3 notes: HTTPS support and FoodKeeper shelf-life auto-suggestion (2026-08-01)

Both shipped together, in the order the author asked for them: "Looks like we need HTTPS support... Pull what references you need from Fiduciary and implement HTTPS support now. Also, go ahead and implement the next item on the project list" (B4.3, picked as the next backlog item after HTTPS, per the standing "author-requested items ship first, then continue down the backlog" pattern).

**B15.1 -- HTTPS.** Read Fiduciary's `portfolio-api/tls.py`/`run_server.py` directly before writing anything, per the author's own instruction. Two deliberate deviations, each documented in its own module docstring: no ACME/Let's Encrypt (tracked as B15.2, needs a public domain this LAN-only deployment doesn't have), and no port of Fiduciary's `store.py`-backed encrypted metadata store (a plain atomic-write JSON file is enough for "which method produced this cert," matching this app's existing lighter-weight patterns elsewhere). The harder problem Fiduciary's own architecture doesn't have: Chef is TWO containers (backend + frontend), and a browser's Secure Context check is about the origin the PAGE itself loaded from, not just the API it calls -- so the frontend needed its own HTTPS listener too, not only the backend.
